## 2026-08-06 — B.12: Milestone B acceptance test — and closing the gap between B.1-B.11's pieces and a real end-to-end analysis pipeline

Writing this test honestly required more than assembling already-built pieces — B.4 through B.9 built the debounce mechanism, the version guard, the bridge encoders, and the Problems pane, but nothing had ever actually *called* `analysis/check`/`analysis/hover` over the wire yet. Closed that gap first:

- `Handlers.handleAnalysisHover` + `dispatch` wiring — the worker side of `analysis/hover` never existed; B.8/B.9 only built the FCS-facing `Hover.hover` function and the bridge's encode side. Added `Protocol.AnalysisHoverParams`/`AnalysisHoverResult`, TDD'd the same way B.2 TDD'd `analysis/check`'s handler.
- `App.AnalysisService` (`check`/`hover`), mirroring `ExecutionService`'s shape — the "host service" layer between a UI call and the worker never existed for analysis, only for execution.
- `ShellViewModel.CheckAsync` + a constructor-time subscription to `ActiveDocument.DebouncedTextChanged` that calls it and applies the result through `ApplyAnalysisResult` — the actual live wiring from "text changed" to "diagnostics appear," which no earlier task actually connected end to end. Required converting `ActiveDocument` from `member val` to a `let` binding (F# requires `let`/`do` bindings to precede all members in a type body, and the constructor's `do` block needs to reference it before any member declarations legally can).
- Wired `CheckAsync` in `App.axaml.fs`'s composition root, and exposed `MainWindow.ShellViewModel` so it could reach it — same "close the gap while writing the acceptance test" precedent as A.19.

**The stale-response race, specifically.** Backgrounded `analysis/check`/`analysis/hover` in `Program.fs`'s read loop (`Task.Run`, same shape as `execution/evaluate`'s existing backgrounding) — before this, the read loop processed every non-evaluate method fully synchronously, meaning two `analysis/check` requests sent back-to-back could *only* ever complete in send order; there was no way for a genuinely older response to arrive after a newer one, making B.5's guard untestable under real concurrency. Unlike `execution/evaluate`, no busy-slot reservation is needed here — each call builds its own isolated `AnalysisContext` in a fresh temp directory already, so concurrent analysis requests don't share mutable state the way `FsiSession` does.

Chose not to chase literal wall-clock racing for the acceptance test itself, though: awaiting two concurrent requests and hoping the older one *happens* to resolve second would be exactly the kind of timing-dependent flake this codebase has avoided elsewhere. Instead the test deterministically controls response *processing* order — awaits and applies the newer (v2) response first, then awaits and applies the older (v1) response second — which is a faithful real request/response round trip through the same worker, "artificially delayed" in the sense the task names (the test chooses when to apply each response), not a mocked guard check like B.5's own unit test.

Also had to bump the "squiggles" scenario's wait from the debounce window alone (400ms) to 3s — a freshly spawned worker's very first `analysis/check` pays FCS's cold reference-resolution cost on top of the debounce, the same first-call latency A.4's DEVLOG entry already flagged for `FsiSession`.

Full solution: 138 tests, all green (`Worker.Tests` 14, `Ui.Tests` 42, `Integration.Tests` 16 — `MilestoneBAcceptanceTests.fs` new, 3 tests, run 3x to confirm no flake).

**Milestone B acceptance criterion met:** invalid F# produces diagnostics with `Error` severity (squiggles, once rendered — B.6's manual-verify boundary), hovering a local symbol resolves its display name and type through a real worker round trip, and a stale analysis response is discarded rather than overwriting newer editor state, proven under a real (not simulated) request/response race enabled by backgrounding analysis requests.

---

## 2026-08-06 — C.1: multi-tab document management on `ShellViewModel`

Added `Documents`/`OpenDocument`/`CloseDocument` to `ShellViewModel`, turning the single `ActiveDocument` slot from A.14 into a real workspace: opening a second script activates a new tab without touching the first's state, reopening an already-open URI reactivates it instead of duplicating a tab, and closing a dirty tab routes through a new `ConfirmDiscardDirtyDocument` hook (same optional-function no-op-when-unconfigured pattern as `ExecuteAsync`/`CancelAsync`) before discarding. Closing the last tab opens a fresh blank one rather than ever leaving `ActiveDocument` unset — every existing caller from Milestone A assumed exactly one always exists.

## 2026-08-06 — C.2: `ConnectorKind`/`ConnectionDefinition` protocol types and `IConnector` abstraction

Added `ConnectionDefinition` to `Protocol`: round-trips through JSON with a `JsonElement` configuration blob and, per Section 10, no credential material at all — only a `SecretReference` pointer, so the shape itself makes "never write a secret to disk" structural rather than a convention to remember later. `IConnector` stays deliberately thin (`Kind`, `TestConnectionAsync`, `DiscoverSchemaAsync`) — query and paging shapes are connector-specific and land with each concrete connector in C.5–C.10, not forced onto one shared interface prematurely.

## 2026-08-06 — C.3: `connection/register` handler and `workspace.json` read/write

Added `Workspace.readConnections`/`writeConnections` (round-trips a `ConnectionDefinition` list through a `workspace.json` file) and `registerConnection`, the `connection/register` handler itself — upsert by `Id`, then persist the full list. Since `ConnectionDefinition` (C.2) only ever carries a `SecretReference` pointer, the written JSON is safe by construction; no redaction step is needed because there's never a secret value in memory to redact at this layer.

## 2026-08-06 — C.4: secret-reference abstraction over the OS credential store

Added `ISecretStore` (`Save`/`TryRead`/`Delete`) to keep `SecretReference` resolution platform-neutral, `InMemorySecretStore` as the fake every unit suite drives, and `WindowsCredentialStore` — P/Invokes `advapi32`'s `CredWriteW`/`CredReadW`/`CredDeleteW` directly for the real Windows credential-manager backing store. Verified against the actual Windows Credential Manager in a `Integration.Tests` case gated by a custom `WindowsOnlyFactAttribute` (xUnit v2 has no built-in dynamic skip, so this had to be written by hand) so the suite stays green on non-Windows CI later. A `Storage.Tests` case proves a registered connection's credential lands in the secret store and never in `workspace.json`, closing the loop with C.3.

## 2026-08-06 — C.5: SQLite connector

Added `SqliteConnector`: `BindParameters` binds every value as a real `SqliteParameter` (unit-tested standalone, no database needed), `ExecuteQuery` reads through a live `SqliteDataReader` lazily per Section 10's reader-based paging rather than materializing the full result set, and `DiscoverSchemaAsync` walks `sqlite_master` plus `PRAGMA table_info` for schema discovery. Bumped the transitive `SQLitePCLRaw.lib.e_sqlite3` dependency past the version NU1903 flagged for `GHSA-2m69-gcr7-jv3q` — a real advisory, not a false positive to suppress.

## 2026-08-06 — C.6: SQL Server connector

`SqlServerConnector` mirrors C.5's shape over `Microsoft.Data.SqlClient`: bound parameters (never concatenated), reader-based lazy row paging, `INFORMATION_SCHEMA`-based schema discovery. Verified against a real containerized SQL Server via `Testcontainers.MsSql` (`ICollectionFixture`, one container shared across the file's tests) — deliberately never a shared/remote service, matching the dev plan's own call-out for this task.

## 2026-08-06 — C.7: PostgreSQL connector

`PostgreSqlConnector` mirrors the same shape again over `Npgsql`, schema discovery scoped to the `public` schema via `information_schema`. Verified against a real containerized PostgreSQL via `Testcontainers.PostgreSql`, same fixture-sharing pattern as C.6.

## 2026-08-06 — C.8: CSV connector

`CsvOptions` covers delimiter/encoding/header choices. Wrote a small RFC4180 streaming parser, `CsvParsing.readRecord`, rather than reaching for `StreamReader.ReadLine`: a quoted field's raw embedded newline isn't a record boundary, so line-based reading would silently corrupt any file with a quoted multi-line value. `ReadRows` streams rows lazily instead of loading the whole file, matching the reader-based-paging spirit of C.5–C.7 even though there's no database reader underneath. `CsvConversions` gives scripts `tryParseInt`/`Float`/`Bool`/`DateTime` over a cell's raw string. Covered with temp fixtures for quoted commas, embedded newlines, escaped `""` quotes, and a non-UTF8 (ISO-8859-1) encoding.

## 2026-08-06 — C.9: Excel connector

`ExcelConnector` (over ClosedXML) selects a target sheet by name or defaults to the first, applies header options the same way `CsvConnector` does, and bounds `ReadRows` to `maxRows` (default 500, matching `Results.Projection`'s preview limit from A.6) rather than materializing an entire worksheet. Cell values keep their natural .NET type (number/boolean/date/text) instead of flattening everything to display strings. `DiscoverSchemaAsync` reports every worksheet as its own table. Covered with a temp `.xlsx` fixture spanning multiple sheets and a header-less sheet.

## 2026-08-06 — C.10: REST/JSON connector

`RestConnector.SendAsync` builds a fully-configured `HttpRequestMessage` (method, query params merged onto the URL, headers, JSON body) and parses the response as `JsonElement`. `PaginateAsync` follows a caller-supplied `extractNextUrl` reader across pages until it returns `None`, since unlike the database/file connectors there's no standardized "next page" shape across arbitrary REST APIs to hardcode against. Schema discovery is intentionally empty for the same reason — no universal way to enumerate "tables" from an arbitrary API. Verified against an in-process `HttpListener` stub bound to loopback, never a real external endpoint. Completes all six first-day connectors (C.5–C.10).

## 2026-08-06 — C.11: controlled script API surface (`Connections.sql`/`csv`/`excel`/`rest`)

Added `Connections`, the only surface a `.fsx` script actually touches to reach a named configured connection — every function dispatches straight to the underlying connector's own bound-parameter API, so a script has no path to interpolate a value into command text through this API even if it tried; the injection-safety guarantee C.5–C.10 already built in at the connector level is now the *only* way a script can query anything. `IConnectionResolver` keeps the module decoupled from how the real registry gets built (`workspace.json` + secret store wiring lands with the composition root later, not here). Regression-tested with two classic SQL-injection payloads (`OR 1=1` and a statement-terminator) against all three SQL-capable connectors — SQLite locally, SQL Server and PostgreSQL against their containers — proving each is treated as literal parameter data, never executed. CSV/Excel/REST routing covered too, completing script-surface coverage across C.5–C.10.

## 2026-08-06 — C.12: virtualized `DataGrid` binding for table results

Replaced A.16's plain `FormattedRows` `ItemsControl` with a real Avalonia `DataGrid` bound directly to `Result.Rows`, which virtualizes rows itself for large result sets — the complexity A.16's own entry explicitly deferred to "once Milestone C's larger result sets make it worth it." `ResultGridColumns.build` is the UI-framework-agnostic piece: maps `ResultEnvelope.Schema` to header/indexed-binding pairs, kept pure and testable since Avalonia needs concrete columns built at runtime (no schema-driven column binding exists in XAML itself). `MainWindow`'s code-behind rebuilds `DataGrid.Columns` whenever `Result.Schema` changes.

Needed `Avalonia.Controls.DataGrid` added to both `Ui` and `App` — `App`'s own AXAML compiler needs the direct package reference to resolve its `StyleInclude` even though `Ui` already pulls it in transitively. Also had to find the theme resource path by trial: it's `.../Themes/Fluent.xaml`, not `.axaml`, despite every other Avalonia resource in this codebase using `.axaml`.

## 2026-08-06 — C.13: `results/page` end-to-end wiring

Added `PagingCursor`, which wraps a live `IEnumerator` and peeks one item past every page so it can answer "is there more" without an extra round trip, while staying the *same* enumerator across calls — deliberately not `Seq.skip`, which restarts a sequence from scratch and would silently re-run a connector-backed query on every page. `Projection.projectWithCursor` hands the cursor out alongside the usual envelope only when a `Table` result gets truncated; the existing `project` (A.6) is unchanged and still drops the cursor, so every prior caller/test keeps working untouched.

`Handlers` now issues a token and stashes the cursor whenever an evaluate truncates, and dispatches `results/page` against it — reusing Section 9's one-evaluation-at-a-time model, since a worker never has more than one open paging cursor at a time. A fresh evaluate clears any prior cursor, so its token stops resolving. An unknown or expired token returns a structured `results/unknownToken` error rather than silently re-running the script. Verified end-to-end against the real worker process: a 510-item sequence truncates at the 500-row preview limit, `results/page` returns the remaining 10, and a bogus token is rejected.

## 2026-08-06 — C.14: CSV export as a worker-side streamed operation

`CsvExport.write` enumerates its row source exactly once, writing one RFC4180-escaped CSV row per pull — never limited to whatever the UI's bounded 500-row preview (A.6/C.9) already fetched, and never requiring the full result to be materialized in memory first. Proved against both a plain in-memory sequence and a real `SqliteConnector` query result (reader-backed, streamed straight through, C.5) exceeding the preview-row limit, confirming the exported file always matches the full source data rather than just what the UI happened to render.

## 2026-08-06 — C.15: copy-to-clipboard for projected results

`ResultViewModel.ClipboardText` projects the current result as tab-separated text (header row first for `Table`, so it pastes cleanly into a spreadsheet) or just `DisplayText` otherwise. `ShellViewModel.CopyResultAsync` sends it through `CopyResultToClipboard`, the same optional-hook no-op-when-unconfigured pattern as `ExecuteAsync`/`CheckAsync`, keeping the view model constructible without a real Avalonia clipboard in tests. `App.axaml.fs` wires it to the window's real `IClipboard`; a "Copy Result" button drives it from the UI.

## 2026-08-06 — C.16: `packages.json` manifest and `#r "nuget: ..."` approval workflow

Added `Packages.readPackages`/`writePackages`/`approvePackage`, mirroring `Workspace`'s shape (C.3) for Section 12's approved-package manifest (name, version, source, resolved assets). `PackageApproval` is the policy layer on top, kept deliberately decoupled from FSI/evaluation entirely: `extractNugetReferences` reads `#r "nuget: ..."` directives straight out of script text and `findUnapproved` diffs them against the manifest, so an approval decision never risks triggering an actual package restore just to check it. `manifestFingerprint` hashes the approved set — any cached session keyed by an earlier fingerprint is stale the moment it changes, satisfying the "requires a session reset" requirement without needing Milestone E's real persistent-session feature to exist yet (every evaluate already gets a fresh session per Section 9, so the fingerprint check itself is what's being proven here).

Verified end-to-end against a real workspace directory: an unlisted `FSharp.Data` reference is rejected, approving it through `Packages.approvePackage` clears the rejection, and the fingerprint changes across that approval.

## 2026-08-06 — C.17: connector call logging — kind, elapsed time, row count, never parameters

Added `IQueryableConnector`, unifying the three SQL-capable connectors (C.5–C.7) behind one interface — each already had its own public `ExecuteQuery`; this just lets `Connections.sql` (C.11), and now logging, reach any of them (or a test fake) without matching on concrete connector types. `Connections.sql` wraps the returned `Rows` in a lazy pass-through that tallies the count and logs kind/elapsed/rowCount only once the caller actually finishes enumerating them, never eagerly — eager counting would force full materialization of what may be a reader-backed sequence, defeating C.5/C.13's reader-based paging entirely just to log a count. Parameter values never touch a log property, verified against an in-memory Serilog sink with a fake connector and a fake "secret-shaped" parameter value that never appears in any captured event.

## 2026-08-06 — C.18: Milestone C acceptance test

One test per connector kind — SQLite, CSV, Excel, and REST self-contained; SQL Server and PostgreSQL reusing their existing container fixtures from C.6/C.7 — each configuring a real connector instance, querying it through its `Connections.*` helper (C.11, the only sanctioned script-facing API), binding the result into a `ResultViewModel` the way `MainWindow`'s `DataGrid` would (C.12), and exporting the full row set via `CsvExport` (C.14). This is the first test in the codebase to drive all six connectors through the identical script-facing path rather than each connector's own lower-level API — closing the loop between C.2's abstraction and C.5–C.10's concrete implementations under one assertion.

Full solution: 248 tests, all green across every project including the Docker-backed SQL Server/PostgreSQL container tests (`Analysis.Tests` 17, `Connectors.Tests` 39, `Editor.Tests` 17, `Execution.Tests` 10, `Integration.Tests` 38, `Logging.Tests` 7, `Protocol.Tests` 9, `Results.Tests` 20, `Security.Tests` 5, `Storage.Tests` 12, `Ui.Tests` 57, `Worker.Tests` 17).

**Milestone C acceptance criterion met:** each of the six supported data targets (SQLite, SQL Server, PostgreSQL, CSV, Excel, REST/JSON) can be configured, queried through its `Connections.*` F# helper with parameters always bound and never concatenated, and displayed/exported through the bounded, paged, virtualized pipeline built across C.1–C.17.

## 2026-08-06 — D.1: `trust.json` read/write

Added `ScriptOrigin`/`TrustState`/`TrustMetadata` to `Protocol` and `Trust.readTrust`/`writeTrust` to `Storage`, mirroring `Workspace`/`Packages`' shape (C.3/C.16) for Section 13's v1 schema: origin, trust state, requested capabilities, optional reviewed-at/reviewed-by. `readTrust` defaults to `Local`/`Unknown` with an empty capability list when `trust.json` doesn't exist yet, rather than throwing — a brand-new workspace has no trust decision made on it yet, same reasoning as `Workspace.readConnections`' empty-list default. This file is read/written/displayed only for v1, per the spec; no enforcement logic exists yet and none is planned before a later milestone.

## 2026-08-06 — D.2: origin detection logic

Added `WorkspaceOpenSource` (`CreatedFresh` / `ExternalPath` / `MarkedShared`) and `OriginDetection.detectOrigin` to `Security`, mapping how a workspace was opened to Section 13's `ScriptOrigin`. The spec names four origin values (local/imported/downloaded/shared-workspace) but only describes three opening scenarios explicitly (fresh, external path, marked shared) — `Downloaded` isn't a fourth input case but a refinement of `ExternalPath`: a path under the OS downloads folder maps to `Downloaded`, everything else under `ExternalPath` maps to `Imported`. Took `downloadsDir` as an explicit parameter rather than resolving it internally via `Environment.GetFolderPath`, so the detection logic itself stays a pure, easily-tested function — the real downloads path is a concern for whatever composition-root code calls this, not this module. `Security` now depends on `Protocol` for `ScriptOrigin`, its first cross-project reference.

## 2026-08-06 — D.3: capability approval UX

Added `ShellViewModel.Trust`/`RequestCapabilityApproval` and a private `TryApproveRun`, gating `RunAsync` on Section 13's trust state before `ExecuteAsync` is ever invoked. `Trusted` always proceeds, `Blocked` never does, and everything else only prompts when `RequestedCapabilities` is non-empty — a script that requests nothing has nothing to approve, so it runs without interrupting the user even while `TrustState` is still `Unknown`. Deliberately inverted the "no-op-when-unconfigured" pattern every other hook on `ShellViewModel` follows (`ExecuteAsync`, `CopyResultToClipboard`, ...): those default to doing nothing when the composition root hasn't wired them up, but `RequestCapabilityApproval` defaults to *denying* — a script requesting capabilities with no way to ask the user must never silently run just because the prompt UI isn't wired yet. Approving flips `Trust.TrustState` to `Trusted` in-memory so the same script doesn't re-prompt on a second `RunAsync` within the session; persisting that approval back to `trust.json` is Storage's job (D.1), not this view model's — `Trust` here is only ever the composition root's already-loaded snapshot.

## 2026-08-06 — D.4: diagnostic-bundle export

Added `DiagnosticBundle.createBundle`/`scanForLeakedSecrets` to `Logging`. `createBundle` zips every `*.log` file under a logs directory last written within a caller-supplied retention window, keyed off a caller-supplied `now` rather than the real clock so bundling stays deterministic in tests. `scanForLeakedSecrets` is the defense-in-depth half the task asked for: a new `Redaction.containsLeakedSecret` regex flags a secret-shaped key (`password`, `token`, `authorization`, ...) assigned to anything *other than* `RedactedPlaceholder` — excluding the placeholder itself is what keeps scanning already-redacted output from flagging its own redaction markers as a leak, while still catching a genuine unredacted value if `RedactingEnricher` ever missed one.

Wrote the scan test two ways on purpose: one seeding a raw unredacted `password=hunter2` fixture (proves the scanner isn't vacuously green), and one running an actual `password` property through a real `RedactingEnricher`-equipped logger before scanning the resulting file (proves A0.6's redaction still holds all the way through Milestone C's code paths). The second version originally called `LoggerSetup.build "host" ...`, which writes to the real process-global `Console.Out` — that raced with `LoggerSetupTests`' own `Console.SetOut`/`SetError` swap once xUnit ran the two test classes in parallel (they're separate default collections), intermittently leaking a stray log line into the other test's captured output. Fixed by building a minimal file-only `LoggerConfiguration` for that test instead of going through `LoggerSetup.build` — no console sink, no shared global state, no race. Worth remembering for any future test that touches `Console.SetOut`/`SetIn`/`SetError`: xUnit parallelizes across test classes by default, so global console redirection anywhere in the suite needs either a dedicated collection or, better, avoiding real Console I/O entirely.

## 2026-08-06 — D.5: dependency reproducibility (locked package hashes)

Added `Hash: string` to `ApprovedPackage` and `PackageLock.computeHash`/`restore` to `Storage`. `computeHash` is a deterministic SHA-256 digest over a package's name, version, source, and sorted resolved assets — a stand-in for a real NuGet content hash, since (per C.16's devlog entry) this app never performs an actual package restore; `restore` recomputes every manifest entry's hash and persists the locked list back to `packages.json`, so calling it twice on an unchanged manifest reproduces byte-identical output.

Adding a mandatory field to an existing record type meant fixing every call site that builds an `ApprovedPackage` literal without one — F# record construction requires every field, so this was a compile-time-enforced list, not a search-and-hope: `PackagesTests.samplePackage`, `Protocol.Tests`' round-trip test, and both `Integration.Tests/PackageApprovalTests.fs` cases. Chose non-optional over `Hash: string option` deliberately: an approved package without a computed hash isn't fully resolved yet, and an `option` would let that half-state silently propagate through `Storage`/`Connectors` instead of being caught at construction.

## 2026-08-06 — D.6: GitHub Actions CI pipeline

Added `.github/workflows/ci.yml`: `windows-latest`, `dotnet restore`/`build --configuration Release`/`test --filter "Category!=RequiresDocker"` against `FSharpQueryStudio.slnx`, on every push and pull request.

The `RequiresDocker` filter needed a decision before writing the workflow: `SqlServerConnectorTests`/`PostgreSqlConnectorTests` (C.6/C.7) and their dependents (`ConnectionsRegressionTests`, `MilestoneCAcceptanceTests`' SQL Server/PostgreSQL classes) spin up real containers via Testcontainers, which needs Linux Docker — not reliably available on GitHub-hosted Windows runners without extra setup. Asked rather than guessed since it decides whether CI is green or flaky-red on day one; the answer was to tag every Docker-backed test class with `[<Trait("Category", "RequiresDocker")>]` (xUnit trait attributes on an F# class apply to every test method inside it) and filter them out in CI, leaving them to run locally same as before — `dotnet test --filter "Category!=RequiresDocker"` locally now runs 26 of Integration.Tests' 38 tests, confirmed against a full solution-wide `dotnet build`/`test --configuration Release` run before ever pushing.

Verification (push + confirm green, then break a test and confirm red) still needs an actual push to trigger a real Actions run — that's a remote-visible, harder-to-undo action, so it's next and deliberately not done inside this commit.

## 2026-08-06 — D.6 follow-up: fix a real concurrency bug and CI-cold-runner timeouts surfaced by the first Actions run

The first pushed run failed with 11 of `Integration.Tests`' 26 tests red — every local run before that push had been solid green, so this was a genuine environment-dependent finding, not a fluke to retry past.

Two distinct causes, both real:

1. **A genuine race in `WorkerClient.Send`**, exposed rather than caused by CI. `ShellViewModel.Cancel` sends its own `execution/cancel` request through `Send` while the original request's `Send` call can still be mid-write on the same process's `StandardInput` — `System.IO.StreamWriter` doesn't support concurrent write operations on one instance, so two overlapping writes threw `InvalidOperationException: The stream is currently in use by a previous operation on the stream.` This is a real production bug (any Run-then-Cancel-quickly from the UI hits the same code path), not a test artifact — it just never lost the race locally because a warm dev machine's timing rarely lines up the two writes closely enough. Fixed with a `SemaphoreSlim(1, 1)` around only the write itself (not the full request/response wait), so a slow response still can't block another request's write from proceeding once its turn comes.
2. **CI-cold-runner timing**, not a bug: several tests (`WorkerClientTests`, `WorkerClientLoggingTests`, `ExecutionServiceTests`, `MilestoneAAcceptanceTests`, `MilestoneBAcceptanceTests`, and `EvaluationTests`' cooperative-cancellation test) hit their local-machine-tuned timeouts/fixed delays on GitHub's shared Windows runner — a cold worker-process spawn plus first-run FCS/FSI JIT is measurably slower there than on a warm dev box. These are safety-net ceilings and a fixed settle delay, not SLA assertions the tests are checking, so raising them (10s→30s, 15s→45s, 20s→60s, 30s→60s, and the squiggles test's fixed 3s wait→10s) doesn't weaken any behavioral assertion — the tests still fail if the behavior itself is wrong, they just stop failing on cold-runner latency alone.

Full solution green locally (including `Category=RequiresDocker` tests) before pushing again.

Second push: CI green (run `31106968351`, 4m9s). D.6's verification called for both a green run and confirmation the workflow goes red on a break — the first push already delivered the red half for real (a genuine race condition, not a staged failure), and the second confirmed green once fixed, so no separate synthetic-breakage push was needed to close this out.

## 2026-08-06 — D.7: Windows packaging/installer (WiX Toolset MSI)

Asked which installer technology before starting — WiX, Velopack, or deferring packaging entirely — since it's a real architectural choice (distribution, signing, long-term maintenance) the plan didn't pin down. Chose WiX Toolset (MSI).

Added `installer/FSharpQueryStudio.Installer.wixproj` + `Package.wxs`, using the modern `WixToolset.Sdk/5.0.2` MSBuild SDK (no separate `wix` CLI tool install needed — `dotnet build` on the wixproj handles restore and compilation like any other SDK-style project) rather than the legacy candle/light toolchain. It harvests every file under the App project's self-contained `win-x64` publish output into `Program Files\FSharpQueryStudio` plus a Start Menu shortcut. `installer/Directory.Build.props` is deliberately empty — it isolates the wixproj from the repo-root `Directory.Build.props` (`net10.0` `TargetFramework`, `TreatWarningsAsErrors`), neither of which applies to a WiX project, and the latter would turn ICE validation warnings into hard failures.

Three real build errors along the way, each with a non-obvious fix:
- **`Files Include="$(PublishDir)**"` silently dropped a leading `..\`** in a relative path, resolving to a nonexistent directory under `installer\` itself rather than the actual publish output — WiX's harvester won't follow `..` past the project directory. Fixed by resolving `PublishDir` to an absolute path via `$([System.IO.Path]::GetFullPath(...))` in the wixproj.
- **"Multiple entry sections" WIX0089/WIX0090**: `WixToolset.Sdk` auto-globs `*.wxs` files by default, and I'd *also* explicitly added `<Compile Include="Package.wxs" />`, compiling the same file twice. Removed the explicit include.
- **ICE80 (459 errors)**: harvested components defaulted to 32-bit while `ProgramFiles64Folder` expects a 64-bit package context. Fixed by setting `<Platform>x64</Platform>` in the wixproj (not on the `<Package>` element itself, which doesn't accept a `Platform` attribute in this schema version — the platform is a project-level MSBuild property here, not part of the `.wxs` markup).

Automated test (`PublishedAppTests.fs`, `Category=RequiresPublish`, deliberately excluded from D.6's CI filter alongside `RequiresDocker` — a full self-contained publish plus a real windowed launch is too heavy for every push, and CI's filter got updated to exclude it too): runs a real `dotnet publish -r win-x64 --self-contained true`, launches the resulting `FSharpQueryStudio.App.exe`, and waits for it to log a new `Host window ready` line — added to `App.axaml.fs`'s `mainWindow.Opened` handler specifically so a black-box test watching the published artifact from outside has *some* signal beyond "the process hasn't crashed yet."

Getting that test itself reliable took three real bugs, not test flakiness to shrug off:
1. Serilog's file sink holds the host log file open with an **exclusive lock for the entire process lifetime**, not just during a flush — confirmed by directly trying to read the file from a second process while the app was running and getting `IOException` every time. A live-polling design (read the file repeatedly while the app runs) can never work against this sink as configured; redesigned to wait a bounded window, kill the process (releasing the lock), *then* read.
2. `File.GetLastWriteTimeUtc`-based filtering to distinguish "this launch's output" from old log content was unreliable — switched to byte-offset diffing (record the file's length before launch, only look at bytes appended after).
3. The real bug that cost the most debugging time: matching `host-*.log` via `Directory.GetFiles` and taking `Array.tryHead` picked *yesterday's* still-retained log file (14-day retention, A0.5) instead of today's active one, because glob enumeration order isn't guaranteed to be newest-first — every check then diffed against a file that was never being written to, so "new text" was always empty no matter what the app logged. Fixed by constructing today's exact filename directly (`host-{yyyyMMdd}.log`, matching `RollingInterval.Day`'s naming) instead of globbing at all.

Verified: `dotnet build installer/FSharpQueryStudio.Installer.wixproj` produces a real 75MB `.msi`; `PublishedAppTests` passes twice in a row locally. MSI installation itself (`msiexec`) wasn't exercised — that needs admin rights and wasn't part of this task's stated verification, which is specifically about the *published artifact* launching and reaching ready, not the installed one.

## 2026-08-06 — D.8: Linux CI matrix for non-UI projects — and a real cross-platform FCS bug it caught

Added a second `ci.yml` job, `build-and-test-linux` (`ubuntu-latest`), matrixed over exactly the nine non-UI test projects Section 14/D.8 name (Protocol, Analysis, Execution, Results, Connectors, Storage, Security, Worker, Integration.Tests), each run individually rather than through `FSharpQueryStudio.slnx` — the solution also contains `Ui.Tests`/`Editor.Tests`/`App`, which this job isn't responsible for. Unlike the Windows job, `RequiresDocker` tests are *not* excluded here: `ubuntu-latest` ships Docker with native Linux container support, so Testcontainers just works — a real coverage gain, since the SQL Server/PostgreSQL tests currently run in neither the Windows CI job nor anywhere else automated.

Validated the whole matrix locally before pushing, using a local Docker container running `mcr.microsoft.com/dotnet/sdk:10.0` rather than trusting it blind — this repo's dev machine happens to have Docker installed, so cross-platform CI changes don't have to be verified for the first time on a real GitHub Actions run. That caught a real, substantive bug, not a test-environment quirk: `AnalysisContext.build`'s call to FCS's `checker.GetProjectOptionsFromScript(scriptPath, sourceText)` relies on FCS's own runtime auto-detection to find the .NET SDK's reference assemblies, and that auto-detection misfires inside a Linux container — `let x : int = "oops"` produced `The type 'int' is not defined` (and unrelated nonsense like a missing `System.Net.WebClient` reference) instead of the expected type-mismatch diagnostic. This wasn't cosmetic: it broke 6 of `Analysis.Tests`' 17 tests, 1 of `Worker.Tests`' 17 (`analysis/hover` dispatch), and 1 of `Integration.Tests`' hover acceptance test — every FCS-backed diagnostic/hover check plus B.8's hover DisplayContext formatting (`"Microsoft.FSharp.Core.int"` instead of `"int"`, since a symbol whose *type itself* doesn't resolve correctly can't format abbreviated either).

Root-caused with a throwaway console app inside the same container, referencing the real `FSharpQueryStudio.Analysis` project directly rather than guessing from FCS docs — confirmed passing `assumeDotNetFramework = false`, `useSdkRefs = true`, `useFsiAuxLib = false` explicitly to `GetProjectOptionsFromScript` fixes both the "int not defined" and the WebClient nonsense, and re-verified the *exact* Windows-vs-Linux hover formatting difference disappears too once the underlying reference resolution is correct — no test needed loosening to tolerate a "platform quirk" that turned out to be a real, fixable resolution bug instead. Applied the fix directly in `AnalysisContext.build` (the only call site); confirmed no regression on Windows (identical results, since these are FCS's own documented flags for SDK-style resolution, not new/different behavior there) and all nine Linux test projects green, including `RequiresDocker`, in the same container setup.

This is the sequencing D.8's own note calls out from the start: without a real Linux job to run against, this bug would have shipped invisibly into whatever later milestone first tries the Linux/macOS UI (E.9) — and would have been much harder to isolate once tangled up with real UI startup issues on an unfamiliar platform, rather than caught here as a clean, reproducible FCS unit-test failure.

## 2026-08-06 — D.9: Milestone D acceptance test

Added `MilestoneDAcceptanceTests.fs`. D.9's synthesized acceptance criterion has three parts, only one of which is a genuine unit-testable behavior — the other two are already covered elsewhere:
- **"An imported/untrusted script shows its requested capabilities and requires explicit approval before running"** — the new test. Wires real `Security.OriginDetection`, `Storage.Trust`, and `Ui.ShellViewModel` together (unlike D.1–D.3's own tests, which each drive one piece in isolation against a fake/literal) to walk the full sequence: detect an `Imported` origin for a workspace path, persist a `trust.json` with requested capabilities, load it back, confirm `RunAsync` refuses to execute with no approval hook wired up, confirm the hook receives exactly the requested capabilities list, confirm approving lets execution through and flips `TrustState` to `Trusted`, then persists that approval back to `trust.json` and confirms a fresh read reflects it — so a second launch of the same workspace won't re-prompt for the same script.
- **"CI is green on a fresh push"** — already true of both `ci.yml` jobs added in D.6/D.8, verified against real GitHub Actions runs (not just locally) throughout this milestone; this commit's own push is one more confirmation, not a new test.
- **"A packaged Windows build launches from a clean machine image"** — D.7's `PublishedAppTests` (`Category=RequiresPublish`) already exercises exactly this against the real self-contained publish output; no reason to duplicate it here.

**Milestone D acceptance criterion met:** trust/capability metadata is real (`trust.json` read/write, origin detection, capability approval UX blocking execution), logs are safe to share (redaction confirmed to hold end-to-end via a diagnostic-bundle scan), and CI exists on both Windows and Linux — the last of which caught and fixed a real cross-platform FCS bug before it could reach a later milestone.

This push's Linux job failed once on `WorkerClientTests.killing the worker process mid-request surfaces a crashed error...` — the same test that passed cleanly on D.8's push moments earlier, with nothing touched in `WorkerClient.fs` or that test file in between. A `gh run rerun --failed` came back fully green with no code changes, confirming a transient timing flake (the test kills the worker process immediately after sending a request, with no synchronization forcing the write and the kill into a specific order) rather than a real regression. Worth a future look if it recurs, but not chased further here — one flake in nine CI runs across this milestone isn't a pattern yet.

## 2026-08-06 — Note: a pasted CI failure that turned out to be a stale run

Got handed a `WorkerClientLoggingTests`/`ExecutionServiceTests` failure log ("timed out after 10s", "stream currently in use") that looked like a fresh pipeline break. It was run `31105659485` — the very first D.6 push (`b6d785d`, 13:23 UTC), from before the `WorkerClient` write-race fix and the CI-cold-runner timeout bumps landed in `00fc504`. Every run from `00fc504` onward, including the D.9 push and this session's most recent one, is green; `main` was already four commits ahead of the failing one. Worth remembering: a CI failure screenshot/log needs its commit SHA checked against current `main` before treating it as live — `gh run list` and `gh run view <id> --json headSha` settle it in one call.

## 2026-08-06 — E.1: opt-in persistent per-tab FSI session mode

First task of Milestone E. Added `SessionStore` to `Execution` — a `ConcurrentDictionary<string, FsiSession.Session>` behind `getOrCreate key workingDirectory` / `reset key` — as the persistence primitive; Isolated mode (Milestone A's default) never touches it, callers build a fresh `FsiSession.Session` directly instead, same as before. `FsiEvaluationSession` has no `Dispose`, so `reset` just drops the dictionary entry and lets the collectible load context get reclaimed once nothing references it — no explicit teardown needed yet.

Extended the protocol to carry the choice: `SessionMode` (`Isolated | Persistent`) and `SessionId: string option` on `EvaluateParams`. Wired `Handlers.runEvaluate` to pick `SessionStore.getOrCreate sessionId workDir` for `Persistent` with a `SessionId`, falling back to an isolated session (logged as a warning, not a hard error) for `Persistent` with none — chose the permissive fallback over a new error code since nothing in the plan calls for rejecting that combination outright, and it's a rarer client bug than a real user-facing case.

The paired Execution.Tests case the plan asked for (`persistent mode: bindings survive across two sequential evaluations under the same session key` / `isolated mode: bindings do not survive across two fresh sessions`) is literally the same assertion shape run through `SessionStore.getOrCreate` vs. two `FsiSession.create` calls — exactly the regression guard against Milestone A's default the task note called for. Mirrored the same behavior one layer up in `Worker.Tests` (`Handlers.handleEvaluate`, two in-process calls) and `Integration.Tests` (`WorkerClient`, two real-worker-process calls) so the persistence actually holds through the full protocol/worker path, not just at the `Evaluation`/`SessionStore` level — the task note only asked for `Execution.Tests`/`Integration.Tests`, but skipping the in-between `Worker.Tests` layer would have left the mode-dispatch `match` in `runEvaluate` itself uncovered by anything faster than a real subprocess test.

Changing `EvaluateParams` from `{ Source: string }` to a 3-field record broke one thing that wasn't visible from the type system: `Integration.Tests/Tests.fs` has a hand-written raw JSON literal (`"""{"source":"..."}"""`) for the A.7-era "throwing script" test, predating the typed helper functions the other test files already use. `Mode` isn't an `option`, so FSharp.SystemTextJson requires it on the wire; the literal's missing `mode` field threw a `JsonException` inside `runEvaluate`'s `try`, which propagated out of the worker's read loop with no response ever written — the client-side `roundTrip` call just sat on its 30s timeout waiting for a reply that was never coming, surfacing as an opaque `System.Exception: timed out after 30s` in the full-suite run rather than an obvious deserialization error near the actual cause. Found the exact wire shape (`{"Case":"Isolated"}` — `JsonFSharpConverter`'s default nullary-union encoding, `PropertyNamingPolicy.CamelCase` applies to the outer record's field names but not to the converter's own `Case` key) with a throwaway failing test that serialized `Isolated` and asserted on the message, rather than guessing; fixed the literal, removed the throwaway test. Worth remembering for any future record change: `git grep` for hand-written JSON literals of the changed type before assuming the typed call sites are the only ones that need updating.

Full solution green: `Protocol.Tests` 10, `Execution.Tests` 15, `Worker.Tests` 18, `Integration.Tests` 41.

## 2026-08-06 — E.2: Reset Session command and session-state display in the UI

The plan's own note for E.2 scopes its tests to `Ui.Tests` only. Asked before starting whether to honor that literally (ViewModel-only scaffolding, `ExecuteAsync` left as `string -> Task<ResultEnvelope>`) or wire it end to end, since the literal reading would have left Persistent mode selectable/displayed in the UI while `App`'s `ExecutionService` kept silently forcing `Mode = Isolated` on every real request — an inert feature, not a deferred one. Chose end-to-end: extended `ShellViewModel.ExecuteAsync` to `EvaluateParams -> Task<ResultEnvelope>` so the active document's `SessionMode` actually reaches the worker, which meant touching `App`/`Integration.Tests` too, beyond the plan's literal note.

`DocumentViewModel.Uri` now doubles as the per-tab `SessionId` a Persistent-mode evaluation is keyed by — it's already stable for the tab's lifetime and unique among open tabs, so no second identifier was needed. Added `SessionMode` (default `Isolated`), `HasActiveSession`, and `SessionStateDisplay` ("Isolated" / "Persistent · active" / "Persistent · reset") to `DocumentViewModel`; switching `SessionMode` clears `HasActiveSession`, since "active" from the old mode doesn't carry meaning into the new one.

Implemented `session/reset` for real rather than leaving `ResetSession` as an inert hook: it was already sitting in Section 7's method table from day one (unimplemented, same as `session/create` still is) with `SessionStore.reset` from E.1 as the ready-made mechanism behind it — `Protocol.SessionResetParams`, `Handlers.handleSessionReset`, and a `Worker.Tests` case that runs a Persistent evaluate, resets, then evaluates again under the same `SessionId` and confirms the binding is actually gone (`ResultKind.Error`, not a stale success) — proving the RPC really drops server-side state, not just that it acknowledges.

`ShellViewModel.RunAsync` now builds `EvaluateParams` from the active document (`SessionId = Some doc.Uri` under Persistent, `None` under Isolated) and calls `doc.MarkSessionActive()` after a successful Persistent run; `ResetSessionAsync()` calls the `ResetSession` hook with the document's `Uri` then `doc.MarkSessionReset()` — both no-ops when their hook isn't configured, the same convention every other `ShellViewModel` hook already follows.

Changing `ExecuteAsync`'s signature broke every test/composition-root call site that pattern-matched its old single-`string` shape — `App.axaml.fs`, `MainWindow.axaml.fs`'s `WireExecution`, `TestHelpers.wireShell`, and five `Ui.Tests`/`Integration.Tests` files with `Some(fun source -> ...)`/`Some(fun _source -> ...)` lambdas. All mechanical renames to `evalParams`/`_evalParams` once the type flowed through; `ExecutionServiceTests`' two `Some(ExecutionService.evaluate client)` call sites needed no change at all, since `evaluate`'s own signature updated in lockstep and partial application just carries the new type through.

Added a `ResetSessionButton` to `MainWindow.axaml` plus a `SessionStateDisplay` text binding, wired the same way `RunButton`/`CancelButton`/`CopyResultButton` already are. No automated test instantiates `MainWindow` directly (it hosts the real `NativeWebView`, same reason A.13 stayed manual-only), so a typo in the `FindControl<Button>("ResetSessionButton")` name wouldn't show up in `Ui.Tests` — ran D.7's `PublishedAppTests` (`Category=RequiresPublish`, not part of the routine full-suite run) explicitly to confirm the real published app still launches and reaches "Host window ready" with the new control in place, rather than trusting the XAML name matched by inspection alone.

Full solution green: `Ui.Tests` 75 (+11), `Worker.Tests` 20 (+2), `Integration.Tests` 41 (unchanged — no new dedicated Integration.Tests case; `ExecutionServiceTests`/`MilestoneAAcceptanceTests` already re-verify the changed `ExecuteAsync`/`ExecutionService` plumbing through `wireShell`), plus a standalone `PublishedAppTests` run confirming the new XAML control.

## 2026-08-06 — E.3: session-invalidation rules on script edit

The plan's own example — "editing an already-executed cell invalidates from that point forward" — is notebook-cell language, but E.4's `// %%` cell parsing doesn't exist yet, and `ShellViewModel.RunAsync` (as of E.2) always sends the whole document as one `Source` string per run. Asked which rule to build against that reality before writing any test, since more than one reasonable answer exists (invalidate on any edit at all; invalidate on anything but a pure append; or defer the policy question entirely and land only the tracking plumbing). Chose prefix-based: a session invalidates unless the new run's source starts with exactly what it last ran. It's the closest textual stand-in for "everything already executed stays valid, only the new tail is unproven" without real cell boundaries, and it matches how the product actually calls `execution/evaluate` today (the whole growing document, not disjoint snippets) — E.4 can later swap the whole-document text for a specific cell's text and reuse the same rule unchanged.

`SessionStore.getOrCreate` grew a required `source` parameter and now tracks a `LastSource` alongside each `Session`; `SessionStore.invalidates lastSource newSource = not (newSource.StartsWith(lastSource))` is exported as its own pure function so `SessionStoreTests.fs` can codify the rule as plain `[<Theory>]` string cases (unchanged / pure append / first-ever-run all valid; edited, truncated, or unrelated text all invalidate) independent of ever spinning up a real `FsiEvaluationSession`. `Handlers.runEvaluate` now threads `evalParams.Source` through to `getOrCreate`, so a request whose text isn't an append of what its `SessionId` last ran transparently gets a fresh session instead of reusing a stale one — no new worker-visible behavior to wire, since the invalidation happens inside the same call that was already creating-or-reusing.

E.1's own persistent-mode tests broke under the new rule and needed real fixes, not just call-site patches: they'd modeled "Persistent mode" as two independent snippets sent back to back (`"let x = 41"` then `"x + 1"`), which is *not* a pure append and would now (correctly) invalidate — closer to a future Run-Selection/notebook-cell workflow than to what `ShellViewModel.RunAsync` actually does today. Rewrote them to send the accumulated whole-document text each round (`"let x = 41"` then `"let x = 41\nx + 1"`), which is both what E.3's rule requires *and* a more faithful test of the real current wiring. Same fix applied to the equivalent `Worker.Tests`/`Integration.Tests` persistence cases from E.1/E.2, which exercise the same `SessionStore.getOrCreate` call path.

Proving invalidation actually *happened* — not just that the rule function returns `true` — needed a three-round test, not two: a two-round "edit invalidates" test can't distinguish "the old session was dropped" from "the edited text just happens to be self-consistent either way," since re-declaring a `let` binding produces the same visible result whether the session is fresh or reused. `EvaluationTests.fs`'s new case defines `x` and `y` in round 1, edits round 2 to drop the `y` line entirely (invalidating), then round 3 appends a bare reference to `y` onto round 2's surviving text — round 3 only fails (undefined `y`) if round 2 actually got a fresh session with no memory of round 1's `y`; a regression that silently kept reusing the old session would make this test fail by round 3 unexpectedly *succeeding*.

Full solution green: `Execution.Tests` 24 (+9: 6 `SessionStoreTests` theory cases plus a fresh no-reuse case, 1 rewritten and 1 new `EvaluationTests` case), `Worker.Tests`/`Integration.Tests` unchanged in count (existing E.1/E.2 cases updated in place, not added to).

## 2026-08-06 — E.4: notebook `// %%` cell parsing and ordered incremental cell execution

Hit a real design conflict with E.3 before writing any code: "later cells see earlier cells' bindings only within a persistent/notebook session" only means something if each cell is sent as its own isolated text per call (relying on the live session to remember earlier cells) — but that's not a prefix-append of the previous call, which is exactly what E.3's `SessionStore.invalidates` rule, built minutes earlier in this same session, was designed to reject as an edit. A cumulative-resend workaround (send `cell1`, then `cell1+cell2`, ...) would have kept `SessionStore` untouched, but Isolated mode would then also "see" earlier cells (they're baked into the resent text every call), which can't actually prove the acceptance criterion. Asked which way to go; chose to generalize `SessionStore` properly.

`SessionStore`'s `Entry` now tracks `LastFragments: string list` instead of a single `LastSource: string` — an ordered execution log, with two different update policies over the same log: `getOrCreate` (whole-document mode, F5 Run, unchanged since E.1/E.3) always replaces the log with `[source]`, applying the existing prefix-invalidation rule against the log's last entry. The new `appendFragment` (notebook/cell mode) always grows the log by one and never invalidates — a genuinely new, unrelated cell isn't an edit of anything already executed, so there's nothing to invalidate; it's what actually lets cell 2 see cell 1's bindings. Both policies share the same underlying `sessions` map, so a key touched by one is visible to the other (tested explicitly: `appendFragment` then `getOrCreate` on the same key reuse the same session, proven with a genuinely new `Cell` type — a check that only makes sense once both code paths share state, not two disconnected mechanisms bolted together).

`FSharpQueryStudio.Analysis.NotebookCells.parse` splits source text at `// %%` boundary lines (bare or titled, e.g. `// %% Cell 2`) into an ordered `Cell list` (0-based `StartLine`, matching `Diagnostic`'s existing Monaco-facing convention). Handles the edge cases a naive split-on-marker would get wrong: a marker as literally the first or last line, or two markers back to back, all correctly produce no empty cell for that gap rather than an empty string masquerading as one; CRLF and LF both normalize the same way. Pure text splitting only — no FSI, no session concept — `Analysis` stays exactly what Section 4 says it is.

Wiring the two together needed no new protocol method, worker handler, or UI command: the plan's own test-location note (`Analysis.Tests`, `Integration.Tests` — no `Worker.Tests`, no `Protocol.Tests`, no `Ui.Tests`) was the tell that E.4 is proving the *mechanism*, not shipping a "Run All Cells" button yet. `NotebookExecutionTests.fs` parses a two-cell script (`let x = 41` / `x + 1`), runs each cell through `SessionStore.appendFragment` + `Evaluation.evaluate` in order under one key, and asserts the second cell's result is `42` — paired, same convention as every other Isolated/Persistent test in this milestone, with a second case that runs the same two cells through independent `FsiSession.create` calls (no shared session) and asserts `x` comes back undefined, proving the first case's success is actually conditioned on session sharing rather than an artifact of the script itself.

Full solution green: `Analysis.Tests` 29 (+12), `Execution.Tests` 28 (+4: `appendFragment` creation/reuse/reset/shares-state-with-`getOrCreate` cases), `Integration.Tests` 43 (+2).

## 2026-08-06 — E.5: `.fsproj`/multi-file workspace support

The architecture doc gives this one line ("add `.fsproj`/multi-file workspace support later") with no schema. Asked the real fork before writing anything: parse a genuine MSBuild `.fsproj` (interoperates with real F# projects someone already has) versus inventing our own JSON manifest (matches `workspace.json`/`trust.json`/`packages.json`'s house style, but doesn't open a real project). Chose real `.fsproj` parsing, since that's what the task's own name implies and there's no reason to compete with an existing, ubiquitous format.

`Storage.FsProj.load` reads a `.fsproj`'s `<Compile Include="...">` items via `XDocument`, filtering by `LocalName = "Compile"` so both SDK-style (no XML namespace) and legacy-style (`xmlns=".../msbuild/2003"`) project files parse identically, and searching the whole document rather than just direct `<ItemGroup>` children so items under `<Choose>`/`<When>` still show up. Deliberately no wildcard/glob support (`<Compile Include="src/*.fs" />`): real F# projects list files explicitly because, unlike C#, compile order is semantically meaningful — the `dotnet new` F# templates never emit globs for exactly that reason, so skipping glob expansion isn't a shortcut so much as matching how F# projects actually get written. "Resolving compile order" ended up being genuinely simple once that was settled: F# has no dependency graph to topologically sort, only a linear order to preserve, so the whole module is document-order preservation plus `resolvedFilePaths` joining each entry onto the project's own directory.

`AnalysisContext.buildForProject` builds an FCS project context over an ordered file list, reusing `build`'s baseline reference resolution (same D.8 explicit-flags fix) rather than re-deriving it, and setting `UseScriptResolutionRules = false` since this is genuine project-mode checking of real on-disk files, not the script-with-override model `build`/`Checker.check` use. Deliberately takes a plain `(projectDirectory, files)` pair rather than `Storage.FsProject` directly — `Analysis` stays independent of `Storage`, matching how every other sibling project here only shares `Protocol`; a caller bridges `FsProj.load` into this. Documented (didn't fix) a real API mismatch this surfaces: `Checker.check` is hardwired to check `context.ScriptPath`, which is a throwaway identity for a multi-file context, not one of the real files — checking a *specific* project file needs a direct `ParseAndCheckFileInProject` call against that file's own path instead. Redesigning `Checker.check` for multi-file callers wasn't part of this task's stated scope (`Storage.Tests`/`Analysis.Tests` only, no `Worker.Tests`/`Protocol.Tests` — same signal E.4 gave for staying mechanism-only) and would need its own design pass.

The Analysis-level test that actually earns its keep here isn't "does `SourceFiles` come out in the given order" (that alone doesn't prove order *does* anything) — it's a real two-file, real-FCS-check pair: `A.fs` defines a value, `B.fs` references it without importing anything; checking `B.fs` against `[A.fs; B.fs]` succeeds with zero diagnostics, and the paired case (same content shape, order reversed so the reference comes *before* its definition) fails with real FCS diagnostics. Same paired-test convention as every other Isolated/Persistent case this milestone, applied here to prove compile order specifically, not an artifact of the file contents.

Full solution green: `Storage.Tests` 25 (+6), `Analysis.Tests` 32 (+3).

## 2026-08-06 — E.6: go-to-definition

Followed B.8/B.9's established shape closely: `Analysis.GoToDefinition.findDefinition` is `Hover.hover`'s same FCS lookup (`GetPartialLongNameEx` + `GetSymbolUseAtLocation`, same 1-based-line/0-based-column FCS convention), just reading `symbolUse.Symbol.DeclarationLocation` instead of type/signature info, mapped back to the protocol's 0-based-line convention the same way `DiagnosticMapping` already does. Protocol got `DefinitionLocation`/`AnalysisDefinitionParams`/`AnalysisDefinitionResult` (mirroring the Hover trio) plus a new `EditorRequest.GoToDefinitionRequested`/`HostNotification.DefinitionResolved` pair on the bridge, correlated by reusing the request's own `EditorEnvelope.Id` as `requestId` — identical convention to `HoverResolved`, not a new mechanism. `Worker.Handlers` got `analysis/definition` alongside `analysis/hover`, and `App.AnalysisService` got a `definition` function mirroring its existing `hover` one (touched anyway by the bug below, and free to add given the exact same shape already existed for hover).

Real bug, not a design question this time: `AnalysisHoverParams` and the new `AnalysisDefinitionParams` are structurally identical (`DocumentUri`/`Version`/`Source`/`Line`/`Column`), and F# resolves an unannotated `{ DocumentUri = ...; Version = ...; ... }` record literal to whichever matching-field-name type was declared *last* in the compilation order — silently reassigning every existing unannotated `AnalysisHoverParams` construction (`AnalysisService.hover`, `HandlersTests.analysisHoverRequest`) to mean `AnalysisDefinitionParams` the moment the new type landed, breaking `SerializeToElement<AnalysisHoverParams>` with an opaque "no overloads match" error nowhere near the actual cause. Same class of bug as E.1's `EvaluateParams`-vs-hand-written-JSON miss, different shape: fixed by adding an explicit `: AnalysisHoverParams` / `: AnalysisDefinitionParams` type annotation on every construction site, not just the new ones — the two `EditorBridge.fs` payloads (`HoverRequestedPayload`/`GoToDefinitionRequestedPayload`, also identical shape) were already written with explicit annotations from the start and never broke, which is exactly what saved them. Worth remembering: any new record sharing an existing type's exact field names needs every unannotated construction site of *both* types checked, not just the new type's own call sites — the compiler won't warn, it just silently repoints old code.

The multi-file case is what actually proves the mechanism, not the same-file one: `GoToDefinitionTests.fs` builds a real E.5 `buildForProject` context over two files (`A.fs` defining `value`, `B.fs` referencing `A.value`), points `findDefinition` at B's real source, and asserts the resolved `DefinitionLocation.FilePath` is `A.fs`'s own absolute path — cross-file navigation, not just a position within one file. Coordinate math bit twice while writing it: "ends at column N" convention meant literally the character *after* the identifier, not any position inside it — clicking column 17 mid-word in `A.value` (on the `l` of "value") returned no symbol at all until moved to column 20, right after the `e`.

Full solution green: `Analysis.Tests` 35 (+3), `Worker.Tests` 22 (+2), `Editor.Tests` 20 (+3).

## 2026-08-06 — E.7: signature help

Third go at the same shape as B.8/B.9/E.6: `Analysis.SignatureHelp.signatureHelp` reuses `Hover`'s exact FCS lookup (`GetPartialLongNameEx` + `GetSymbolUseAtLocation`), reading `CurriedParameterGroups`/`ReturnParameter` off the resolved `FSharpMemberOrFunctionOrValue` instead of `FullType`. Deliberately click-on-the-identifier, same as Hover/GoToDefinition — not cursor-anywhere-inside-an-argument-list with an active-parameter index, which needs walking up the enclosing call expression to find which method is even being invoked (FCS's real API for that, `GetMethods`, returns a `MethodGroup` of `MethodGroupItem`/`MethodGroupItemParameter` with `TaggedText[]` display fragments, materially more machinery than reusing the existing single-symbol path). Noted as a scope choice, not silently punted: a full cursor-tracking version is a real design step of its own for whenever the UI actually needs it, not something to half-build now.

`FSharpMemberOrFunctionOrValue.ReturnParameter`/`FSharpParameter.Name` (an `option`, not `DisplayName`) aren't in FCS's own XML doc comments (present in the assembly, just undocumented) — confirmed both by reflecting the live loaded type at test time rather than guessing from partial docs, the same move used earlier in this session to pin down `SessionMode`'s exact JSON wire shape.

Applied E.6's lesson before it could repeat: `AnalysisSignatureHelpParams`/`SignatureHelpRequestedPayload` share the same field names as their Hover/Definition counterparts, so every construction site got an explicit type annotation from the very first write, not added after a build error. Also added the App-level `AnalysisService.signatureHelp` alongside `hover`/`definition` for the same reason `definition` got one in E.6 — identical shape, no new design question, and touching this file's pattern once means matching it consistently rather than leaving `hover`/`definition` with App wiring and `signatureHelp` without.

Full solution green: `Editor.Tests` 23 (+3), `Analysis.Tests` 38 (+3), `Worker.Tests` 24 (+2).

## 2026-08-06 — E.8: rename/refactoring support

Lighter than E.6/E.7: the task's own note names only `Analysis.Tests` (no `Editor.Tests` bridge contract this time) — no protocol/bridge/worker wiring belongs in this task, just the underlying capability. `Analysis.Rename.findReferences` does the same symbol lookup as `Hover`/`GoToDefinition`/`SignatureHelp`, then calls `FSharpCheckFileResults.GetUsesOfSymbolInFile` on the resolved symbol to get every reference within the checked scope — declaration included, not just the site clicked. Deliberately returns *locations* only, never rewritten text: `Analysis` stays read-only everywhere else in this project (Section 4), and applying edits at each returned range is a caller concern, not something worth breaking that separation for.

`RenameEdit`/`findReferences` live in `Analysis` itself, not `Protocol` — no wire boundary needs crossing yet since nothing outside `Analysis` calls this in this task, unlike `DefinitionLocation`/`SignatureHelpInfo` which had to be protocol types the moment a worker RPC needed to carry them. Matches "don't build ahead of the current task": adding `Protocol`/`Worker` surface for a bridge that doesn't exist yet would be exactly that.

The acceptance criterion ("updates every reference … not just the declaration") only has teeth against a symbol actually used more than once — `RenameTests.fs` uses `total` declared once and referenced twice, asserting `findReferences` returns 3 edits (declaration + both uses) whether clicked from the declaration or from either use site, plus a single-reference control case (declared, never used elsewhere) asserting exactly 1 edit, so the count itself is meaningful rather than always trivially 1.

Full solution green: `Analysis.Tests` 42 (+4).

## 2026-08-06 — E.9: official Linux/macOS UI support (CI matrix, no refactor needed)

E.9's own note says it "starts with a refactor" if the WebView/editor-host boundary hasn't stayed clean through A–D. Checked first, read-only, before touching anything: `NativeWebView` (`Avalonia.Controls.WebView`) is Avalonia's own cross-platform control — not a Windows-only WebView2 wrapper, no `#if WINDOWS`, every UI-adjacent project (`Ui`, `Editor`, `App`) targets plain `net10.0` (inherited from the root `Directory.Build.props`), not `net10.0-windows`. The only Windows-specific artifact anywhere is `App.fsproj`'s `app.manifest` (a Win32 manifest, inert on other platforms). So: no refactor needed — the boundary held.

What *was* stale: D.8's own Linux job comment claimed "Ui/Editor/App stay Windows-only (WebView2)," and no test anywhere (not `Ui.Tests`, not `Integration.Tests`) ever actually constructs a real `MainWindow`/WebView — `Ui.Tests` only exercises plain ViewModels (`ShellViewModel`, `DocumentViewModel`, etc., no XAML controls involved at all), so the "WebView2-only" premise had never actually been tested, just asserted in a comment.

Asked how deep to take "validate on a second platform" before doing anything, since two very different scopes were both defensible: add the existing headless `Ui.Tests`/`Editor.Tests` to the Linux CI matrix as-is (cheap, proves the code *compiles and the ViewModel layer runs* cross-platform), versus building a real Linux equivalent of D.7's `RequiresPublish` test — an actual `linux-x64` publish launched under Xvfb with WebKitGTK, proving Monaco genuinely renders through a real Linux WebView. Chose the former: it's what's actually verifiable without open-ended CI-environment iteration, and the DEVLOG says so plainly rather than leaving "Linux/macOS UI support" sounding more validated than it is — a real windowed Linux launch test remains a known, explicitly flagged gap, not something silently declared done.

Confirmed both suites pass in a real `mcr.microsoft.com/dotnet/sdk:10.0` Linux container before touching CI at all (same move as D.8, which caught a real bug that way) — `Ui.Tests` 75/75, `Editor.Tests` 23/23, zero code changes needed on either side. Also confirmed `App` itself (`dotnet build`, no dedicated test project per Section 4's own exception) succeeds in the same container, though it isn't part of the test matrix.

Added `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests` and `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Editor.Tests` to `ci.yml`'s Linux matrix, renamed the job from "Linux, non-UI" to "Linux" since that qualifier no longer applies, and rewrote the stale comment to state what's actually been verified versus what's explicitly still open (the real windowed WebKitGTK launch).

## 2026-08-06 — E.10: Milestone E acceptance test

`MilestoneEAcceptanceTests.fs` covers three of the synthesized criterion's four parts as genuine behavioral tests, reusing only mechanisms their own milestone tasks already built - same approach D.9's acceptance test took, not a fresh integration invented for this test alone. The fourth ("the UI runs on at least one non-Windows platform in CI") is E.9's Linux matrix change itself, verified against a real GitHub Actions run rather than a unit test.

Two of the three tests were wrong on first pass, both for the same underlying reason: reusing this session's own recent work without re-deriving what it had actually proven.

1. **Persistent-session-until-reset**, through a real worker (extended `TestHelpers.wireShell` to also wire `ResetSession`, matching the real `App` composition root — the only source change this task needed). First draft asserted `ResultKind.Scalar` on a bare `let x = 41`'s own result; failed with `Text` instead. A bare `let` produces no expression value — `EvaluationTests.fs` already characterizes this (Milestone A), the acceptance test just didn't carry that forward. Fixed to assert non-`Error` instead, which is what the step actually needs to prove.

2. **Notebook cell-by-cell execution** — first draft sent each cell as its own real `execution/evaluate` request through `WorkerClient`, reasoning that the milestone acceptance test should exercise the real worker path. It failed with the second cell (`"x + 1"`) coming back `Error`: `Handlers.runEvaluate` only ever calls `SessionStore.getOrCreate` (E.3's whole-document revise-in-place policy, which requires each call's source to be a prefix-append of the last), never `SessionStore.appendFragment` (E.4's notebook policy) — there is no protocol/worker path that lets a client select cell-append mode. E.4's own scope was deliberately mechanism-only, proven in-process; this test's first draft quietly assumed a worker-level capability that was never built. Rewrote it to match `NotebookExecutionTests.fs`'s actual layer (`SessionStore.appendFragment` + `Evaluation.evaluate`, in-process) instead of inventing new worker wiring under an acceptance test's cover — extending `execution/evaluate` to support cell-append mode is real, undone scope for a future task, not something to smuggle in here.

3. **Multi-file `.fsproj` analysis** passed on the first try: a real `Project.fsproj` on disk listing `A.fs`/`B.fs`, loaded via `FsProj.load`, checked via `AnalysisContext.buildForProject`, `B.fs`'s reference to `A.value` resolving with zero diagnostics purely from `.fsproj` compile order.

Pushed after E.9's CI-matrix change (9 commits queued locally at that point — E.1 through E.9 had accumulated without a push) specifically to verify the new Linux `Ui.Tests`/`Editor.Tests` jobs for real, not just against local Docker.

Full solution green locally: `Integration.Tests` 46 (+3, all of Milestone E's own accumulated total this session).
