Eleven new `WorkspaceConnectionViewModelTests.fs` cases covering `Label` composition, `HasSchema` per connector kind, the idempotent-on-success/retriable-on-failure load semantics, `PropertyChanged` firing, and `UpdateDefinition` reconciliation. Six new `ShellViewModelTests.fs` cases for `WorkspaceItems` construction, instance reuse across a second `SeedConnections`, add/remove tracking, `PropertyChanged`, and the `LoadConnectionSchemaAsync` hook actually reaching a `WorkspaceConnectionViewModel` built by `SeedConnections`.

**Verification, and its real limit.** `dotnet build` clean across the full solution (0 warnings, 0 errors) — this is itself real signal for the XAML: `Expanding="ConnectionExpanderExpanded"` and every `{Binding ...}` path against `WorkspaceConnectionViewModel`/`WorkspaceTableViewModel`/`WorkspaceColumnViewModel` are resolved by the XAML compiler at build time, so a typo in a binding path or a missing event-handler member would have failed the build. `Ui.Tests` 118 → 137, `Worker.Tests` 39 → 41. Full-solution `dotnet test`: everything green except the same 12 pre-existing `RequiresDocker` `Integration.Tests` failures. What's *not* verified: what this actually looks like or feels like to use — expanding a real connection, watching "Loading…" replace itself with real tables, drilling into a table to see real columns. `claude-in-chrome` was unavailable for the entire session (same standing limitation noted in every `editor-host.html`/XAML entry above); this is the first feature this session that's pure Avalonia XAML with no prior visual check at all, so the gap is larger here than for `editor-host.html`, where at least a structural harness existed. Flagged honestly rather than claimed as done.

Files changed: `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Protocol/Connections.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Connectors/ExcelConnector.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Connectors/CsvConnector.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Connectors/PostgreSqlConnector.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Connectors/SqlServerConnector.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Worker/Handlers.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.App/ConnectionService.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.App/App.axaml.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/WorkspaceConnectionViewModel.fs` (new), `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/ShellViewModel.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/MainWindow.axaml`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/MainWindow.axaml.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.fsproj`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Worker.Tests/HandlersTests.fs`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests/WorkspaceConnectionViewModelTests.fs` (new), `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests/ShellViewModelTests.fs`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests.fsproj`.

## 2026-08-11 — Fix: Remove button silently broken by the schema-navigation XAML rework; UI polish for the Workspace panel

User request, two small UI adjustments on top of the just-shipped schema navigation: make each connection row occupy the panel's full width even when expanded, and add a way to view/edit a connection's properties from its row.

**Real bug found while touching this code, not requested but fixed anyway.** The previous entry's XAML rework changed `WorkspacePanel`'s `ItemsSource` from `Connections` (`ConnectionDefinition list`) to `WorkspaceItems` (`WorkspaceConnectionViewModel list`), but `MainWindow.RemoveConnectionClicked` still matched the clicked button's `DataContext` against `:? ConnectionDefinition` — a type that row's `DataContext` can never be anymore. The "×" button had been a complete, silent no-op since that commit: no exception, no log, the click handler's `match` just fell through its `_ -> ()` case every time. Caught only because implementing the new edit button required reading this exact code and noticing the type mismatch. Fixed by matching `WorkspaceConnectionViewModel` and calling `shellViewModel.RemoveConnection vm.Definition`.

**Full width.** Avalonia's `Expander` and `ItemsControl` don't stretch to fill their parent by default in this theme — each outer/inner `Expander`, its header `Grid`, and the `WorkspaceItemsControl`/table `ItemsControl` itself now carry explicit `HorizontalAlignment="Stretch"` (`ItemsControl` in this Avalonia version has no `HorizontalContentAlignment` of its own — confirmed by a real `AVLN2000` compiler error when first tried, not assumed).

**View/edit connection properties.** The "New Connection" dialog builder (`showNewConnectionDialog`, wired to `NewConnectionMenuItem`) is now `showConnectionDialog (existing: ConnectionDefinition option)`, reused for both: `None` for a brand-new connection, `Some existingDefinition` for a row's new pencil button (`EditConnectionClicked`, next to the existing "×"). Editing reuses `RegisterConnection`'s own replace-by-`Id` behavior (`Workspace.registerConnection` already replaces-if-`Id`-matches) — same call, same `Id`, dialog fields pre-filled by reading back `Name`/`Kind`/`Configuration`'s `FilePath`/`ConnectionString`/`Url` property. The password field is deliberately never pre-filled (`ConnectionDefinition` only ever carries a `SecretReference` pointer, never the plaintext) — left blank while editing a connection that already has a secret, the existing reference (and the credential it points at) is untouched; typed over, it's saved under the same reference if one already existed, or a freshly minted one otherwise. Dialog title/button text switch between "New Connection"/"Register" and "Connection Properties"/"Save".

**A structural constraint specific to this fix.** `showConnectionDialog` is a closure defined inside `MainWindow`'s constructor `do` block (needs `this` for `ShowDialog(this)`, built at that point in construction) — invisible to ordinary class members declared outside that block, unlike top-level primary-constructor `let`s (e.g. `shellViewModel`), which do become instance-visible fields. `EditConnectionClicked`, like `RemoveConnectionClicked`/`ConnectionExpanderExpanded` before it, has to be a real class member (XAML's declarative `Click="..."` needs one, not a closure — the recurring reason across all three per-row handlers, since each row's controls are separate dynamically-created instances with no name `FindControl` could reach). Bridged with a mutable hook field, `showConnectionDialogHook`, set once inside the `do` block to the real closure — same no-op-until-configured shape as every `ShellViewModel` hook, except here "configured" happens synchronously during construction rather than by the composition root.

Not unit-tested — same standing limitation as every other `MainWindow.axaml`/`.axaml.fs` change in this codebase; verified by a clean `dotnet build` (the XAML compiler resolves every `{Binding ...}` path and `Click`/`Expanding` handler name against the real types/members at build time) and the full existing `Ui.Tests` suite staying green (confirms `ShellViewModel.RemoveConnection`/`RegisterConnection`, which `RemoveConnectionClicked`/the dialog's save handler call into, are unaffected). Not visually verified — `claude-in-chrome` unavailable this session, same limitation as the previous entry.

Verified: `dotnet build` clean across the full solution (0 warnings, 0 errors) once a locally running `FSharpQueryStudio.App` instance — holding a file lock on `Ui`/`Worker`/`Logging` DLLs the App project needed to copy — was closed. `Ui.Tests`: 137 → 137 (no test changes this entry; the remove-button fix and the dialog rework are both covered only by the existing suite's coverage of the `ShellViewModel` methods they call, not by new tests of their own, since the code that changed is entirely in untestable XAML/code-behind).

Files changed: `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/MainWindow.axaml`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/MainWindow.axaml.fs`.

## 2026-08-11 — Feature: each tab gets its own Results/Output/Schema, closing the gap flagged in a prior commit

User request: "keep the bottom menu connected to the tab session, so for each tab we should have itself result, output, problems and schema." This is exactly the gap an earlier commit this session (`Docs: note that Results/Output/Schema aren't scoped per tab`) recorded rather than fixed: `Problems` was already correctly derived from the *active* document's own `Diagnostics`, but `ShellViewModel.Result` was a single `member val Result = ResultViewModel()` — one shared instance for the whole workspace, so `ResultsView`'s Results/Output/Schema tabs (all three bind through `Result.Kind`/`Result.Output`/`Result.Schema` — one `ResultViewModel` genuinely owns all three, `ResultsView.axaml` confirmed) showed whichever tab last ran a script, regardless of which tab was actually active.

**Fix, mirroring `Problems`'s existing shape exactly.** `DocumentViewModel` gained its own `Result: ResultViewModel`, constructed once per document alongside its `Diagnostics`. `ShellViewModel.Result` changed from an auto-property to `member this.Result = this.ActiveDocument.Result` — computed on every access, never cached, same as `Problems`. A `raiseResultChanged()` helper (`PropertyChanged("Result")`) fires at the exact three places `raiseProblemsChanged()` already does — `OpenDocument`'s tab switch, and both of `CloseDocument`'s reactivation branches — so `{Binding Result.Xxx}` in `ResultsView.axaml` re-resolves the moment the active tab changes, the same "derived property notifies nothing on its own" fix already established for `Problems`.

**The one real race this touched.** `RunAsync` used to apply its envelope via `this.Result.Apply(envelope)` — reading `ActiveDocument` fresh at that point. Now that `Result` is per-document, that would have applied a slow run's result to whichever tab happens to be active *when the response lands*, not the tab that was actually run — exactly the class of bug `RouteTextChanged`'s own doc comment already describes and guards against for text changes. Fixed by capturing `doc = this.ActiveDocument` once at the top of `RunAsync` (it already did this, for `EvaluateParams`/`MarkSessionActive`) and applying the envelope to `doc.Result` instead of `this.Result` — so a background run that finishes after the user has switched tabs still lands on the right document, silently, and the tab the user is actually looking at is left alone. A new test drives exactly this: start a run via a pending `TaskCompletionSource`, switch tabs while it's in flight, complete it, and assert the result landed on the *original* document while the newly-active one still shows `NoValue`.

**Code-behind half: the Results grid's columns.** `ResultsView.axaml`'s `DataGrid` columns aren't declarative (`ResultGridColumns.build` constructs `DataGridTextColumn`s in code, per C.12), so `MainWindow.axaml.fs` used to subscribe once, at construction, to the one global `ResultViewModel`'s `PropertyChanged` for `"Schema"` — a subscription to a single fixed object, which would have kept rebuilding columns for the *first* tab's `ResultViewModel` forever once `Result` became per-document, never noticing a switch to any other tab. Replaced with two things: (1) `rebuildResultColumns()` moved earlier in the constructor and called directly from the already-existing `ActiveDocument`-changed subscription, so switching tabs always re-renders the grid's columns for whatever tab just became active; (2) the three places that invoke a run (`RunButton`, `RunMenuItem`, the F5 `KeyBinding`) now go through a shared `runAndRebuild()` that awaits `RunAsync()` and calls `rebuildResultColumns()` once it completes, reading `shellViewModel.Result.Schema` fresh at that moment — correct even if the user switched tabs mid-run, since it always reflects whatever's *currently* active, never the (possibly different) document the just-finished run actually belonged to.

TDD: two new `DocumentViewModelTests.fs` cases (a fresh document's `Result` starts at `NoValue`; two documents never share a `Result` instance — `Assert.NotSame`). Five new `ShellViewModelTests.fs` cases: `Result` is literally `ActiveDocument.Result`; switching tabs shows each tab's own previously-applied result untouched by the other; the mid-flight-tab-switch race above; `PropertyChanged` fires for `Result` on both `OpenDocument` and `CloseDocument` tab switches (mirroring the existing `Problems` cases one-for-one). The `DocumentViewModel`/`ResultViewModel` compile-order dependency this introduced (`DocumentViewModel` now references `ResultViewModel`, previously the reverse wasn't required either way) needed `FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.fsproj`'s `<Compile>` order swapped — caught immediately as a real compiler error, not guessed at.

Verified: `dotnet build` clean across the full solution. `Ui.Tests` 137 → 144 (7 new, all passing). `Worker.Tests` unaffected, still 41 green. Not visually verified — `claude-in-chrome` unavailable this session, same standing limitation as every other XAML-touching entry above.

Files changed: `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/DocumentViewModel.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/ShellViewModel.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/MainWindow.axaml.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.fsproj`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests/DocumentViewModelTests.fs`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests/ShellViewModelTests.fs`.

## 2026-08-11 — Merge: reconciled with an independent fix for the same gap built directly on `main`

Merging this branch into `main` surfaced a real collision: `main` had picked up its own commit (`Fix: Results/Output/Schema now scoped per tab, not shared window-wide`) closing the exact same gap the previous entry describes, built independently and concurrently, on top of the pre-Connections-UI `ShellViewModel` (`main` never had the Connections/schema-navigation work — it only exists on this branch). Same root cause identified, same fix shape (`Result` moved onto `DocumentViewModel`, `ShellViewModel.Result` derived from `ActiveDocument`, `RunAsync` applying to the captured `doc` instead of a freshly-read `this.ActiveDocument`), arrived at independently on both sides.

Resolved by keeping this branch's implementation (a strict superset — `main`'s single commit sat on code that predates all of this branch's Connections/LSP/schema-navigation work), with one deliberate adoption from `main`'s side: its `wireDocument` listens on each document's own `Result.PropertyChanged("Schema")`, gated by `ReferenceEquals(activeDocument, doc)`, re-raising `ShellViewModel`'s own `PropertyChanged("Result")` — one signal that covers both a tab switch *and* the active tab's own result changing after a run. That's simpler than this branch's original approach (a `runAndRebuild` wrapper duplicated across the Run button, Run menu item, and F5 key binding, each explicitly re-rendering the grid after `RunAsync` completed); adopted it and reverted those three call sites back to plain `RunAsync() |> ignore`, letting the single `shellViewModel`-level `"Result"` subscription in `MainWindow.axaml.fs` handle the DataGrid column rebuild for both triggers.

Test suites reconciled the same way — kept this branch's `ShellViewModelTests` (which already covered tab-switch isolation and the mid-flight race) and added two cases unique to `main`'s side: `PropertyChanged fires for Result when the active document's own result changes` and `PropertyChanged does not fire for Result when a background document's result changes`, both needed to actually exercise the adopted `wireDocument` bridge.

Verified: `dotnet build` clean across the full solution after resolving conflicts in `ShellViewModel.fs`, `DocumentViewModel.fs`, `MainWindow.axaml.fs`, `ShellViewModelTests.fs`, and this file. `Ui.Tests`: 144 → 146 (the two adopted from `main`'s side). `Worker.Tests` unaffected by this merge.

## 2026-08-12 — Feature: in-app log viewer — a new `_Help` menu, live-tailed Host+Worker logs, past-day browsing, Level/Process filters

User request: "a log visualization for the use of the app to have a clear view of what's going on." `LoggerSetup.build` (Section 15) has written daily-rolling `host-YYYYMMDD.log`/`worker-YYYYMMDD.log` files to `%LOCALAPPDATA%/FSharpQueryStudio/logs/` since early in this project, correlation-tagged and redacted before any event reaches a sink, and `DiagnosticBundle.fs` (D.4) can already zip them up for sharing — but nothing in the running app could show them. A user chasing a problem had to know the folder existed and open the raw files in a text editor. Built as five tasks off a design spec and implementation plan (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-12-log-viewer-design.md`, `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-12-log-viewer.md`), each reviewed as it landed and each approved clean on first or second pass — see `.superpowers/sdd/2026-08-12-log-viewer/progress.md`.

**Why the file sinks switched to JSON.** The existing sinks used Serilog's default plain-text template — fine for a human tailing a file, but exactly the kind of fragile parsing this codebase has avoided elsewhere (`AnalysisContext`'s line-offset handling, `DiagnosticMapping`'s explicit coordinate conversion): regex-scraping a human-readable template to recover level/time/process/message reliably. `LoggerSetup.build`'s `WriteTo.File(...)` call (`src/FSharpQueryStudio.Logging/LoggerSetup.fs:50-57`) now passes `Serilog.Formatting.Json.JsonFormatter(renderMessage = true)` instead of the default formatter — `renderMessage = true` is what makes each JSON line carry a ready-to-display `RenderedMessage` string with property values already substituted, rather than only the raw `{Property}`-templated `MessageTemplate` a caller would have to re-render by hand — plus `shared = true`, so the Host process's log viewer can open the Worker's own log file for reading while the Worker still holds it open for writing (Serilog's file sink defaults to an exclusive lock otherwise, which would have made "read the Worker's own log while it's running" outright impossible). `RedactingEnricher` runs upstream of any sink (it mutates `LogEvent.Properties` before formatting), so redaction is unaffected by the formatter change — confirmed by a Task 1 test asserting a `password`-shaped property still comes back as the redaction placeholder, not the original value, after round-tripping through the new formatter. Only the *file* sinks changed; the console sink (visible only when running from a terminal, not the packaged app) is untouched.

**Reading side.** `FSharpQueryStudio.Logging` gained a `LogEntry` record (`Timestamp`/`Level`/`ProcessRole`/`Message`) and a `LogFileReader` module, parallel to `DiagnosticBundle.fs`'s existing role as this project's "read side" of logging. `parseLine` (Task 1) parses one JSON line into a `LogEntry`, returning `None` — never an exception — for anything that isn't a complete, well-formed event; a line caught mid-write (the live-tail poller reading a partial JSON fragment the instant it was flushed) is an expected, routine case here, not an error, and the next poll simply re-reads the same bytes once the line is whole. `listAvailableDates` (Task 2) scans a directory for `host-*.log`/`worker-*.log` filenames, returning their embedded dates deduplicated and newest-first, for the date picker; empty (not an error) for a directory that doesn't exist yet. `readEntriesSince filePath byteOffset` (Task 2) is the piece that makes live-tail polling cheap: it seeks to `byteOffset`, reads only the bytes appended since then, parses complete lines, and returns the new offset alongside them — a trailing line with no terminating `\n` yet is left unconsumed, so the offset stops before it and the next call picks it up once it's whole, instead of re-reading and re-parsing the whole file on every tick. `readAllEntries` is `readEntriesSince filePath 0L |> fst`, used for a past date's one-time static read.

**The poll-based live tail, and why not `FileSystemWatcher`.** `LogViewerViewModel.Poll()` (Task 3) is driven by a real Avalonia `DispatcherTimer` at a roughly one-second interval, started when `LogViewerWindow` opens and stopped on `Closed` (`src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/LogViewerWindow.axaml.fs:24-27`) — not a `FileSystemWatcher`-driven push, a real alternative that was considered and explicitly rejected in the design spec. At this data volume (a handful of log lines per second at most, from a two-process desktop app, not a high-throughput service) polling is simpler and just as reliable: no watcher-thread lifecycle to manage alongside the window's own lifetime, no platform-specific quirks around `FileSystemWatcher` coalescing or dropping rapid-fire change events (a documented, real limitation of the Win32 API it wraps), and `readEntriesSince`'s byte-offset tracking already makes each poll cheap — a tick that finds nothing new does one small seek-and-read per file, not a full re-scan. `Poll()` tracks the last-read offset per file (host/worker) internally, merges any new entries from both into the existing sorted set, and is a deliberate no-op whenever `IsLive` is `false` (viewing a past date) — so the window's timer can call it unconditionally on every tick without the window itself needing to know or care whether a live view is currently showing. Any read exception inside `Poll()` — a transient sharing violation despite `shared = true`, disk pressure — is caught and swallowed for that tick; the log viewer exists to help debug the running app, so it must never be able to take that app down itself, and the next tick simply tries again.

**`string`-with-`"All"`-sentinel filters, not `option`, and why.** `LevelFilter`/`ProcessFilter` on `LogViewerViewModel` (`src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/LogViewerViewModel.fs:16-19`) are plain `string`, narrowing `Entries` against the literal sentinel `"All"` rather than `string option` narrowing against `None`. This was a deliberate design-time constraint, not an oversight discovered mid-task — the design spec's original sketch used `string option`, and the implementation plan overrode it before Task 3 ever started a line of code, citing two real incidents already in this same file. The first: the `"Some(null)"` display bug (2026-08-11, "`Results.table`/`Results.text`... fixes the 'Some(null)' display bug at its root", above) — `ResultViewModel.DisplayText` bound a raw `string option` straight to a XAML `TextBlock.Text`, and Avalonia can't implicitly convert `option` to `string` for that binding, so it silently fell back to calling `.ToString()` on the boxed `FSharpOption<string>` itself, rendering the literal text `"Some(null)"` instead of the string it wrapped. The second: `WorkspaceConnectionViewModel.LoadError` (2026-08-11, "real object navigation for the Workspace panel", above), which was deliberately exposed only as an already-unwrapped `HasError`/`ErrorMessage` pair rather than the raw option, "following the `ResultViewModel.DisplayText` lesson from earlier this session — binding a raw `string option` straight to a XAML `TextBlock.Text` falls back to Avalonia calling `.ToString()` on the boxed `FSharpOption<string>`, rendering the literal text `"Some(...)"`/`"None"` instead of unwrapping it." Two hits of the identical bug shape in one session was enough to make it a standing rule for this feature rather than something to rediscover a third time: `LevelOptions`/`ProcessOptions` both start with the `"All"` sentinel, `LevelFilter`/`ProcessFilter` default to it, and `Entries`' filter predicates (`levelFilter = "All" || levelFilter = e.Level`) never touch `Option.map`/`Option.defaultValue` or a bindable option at all — there is nothing here for the bug to recur against.

**The window.** `LogViewerWindow.axaml` (Task 4) is a non-modal `Window` — a `DataGrid` (Time/Level/Process/Message columns, the same hand-built `DataGridTextColumn` pattern `ResultGridColumns`/`MainWindow`'s own results grid already use, though here declared directly in XAML since the columns are fixed rather than schema-driven) plus a date-picker `ComboBox` bound to `AvailableDates`/`SelectedDate` and two filter `ComboBox`es bound to `LevelOptions`/`LevelFilter` and `ProcessOptions`/`ProcessFilter`, all direct bindings (`x:CompileBindings="False"`, the same convention every other view in this project uses). A `HasNoEntries`-gated "No logs yet" placeholder covers the freshly-installed-app case. Both files were used verbatim from the brief with no changes needed — every binding path resolved cleanly against `LogViewerViewModel`'s real members on the first build.

**Task 5 (this entry): wiring it in.** `MainWindow.axaml` gained a new top-level `_Help` menu (`src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/MainWindow.axaml:43-45`, sitting after `_Run`) with a single `_Logs…` item — the design spec notes a second item will land here once the Welcome/user-guide sub-project of this same observability-and-polish arc ships. `MainWindow.axaml.fs` wires it with one line, right alongside `NewConnectionMenuItem`'s own wiring: `this.FindControl<MenuItem>("LogsMenuItem").Click.Add(fun _ -> LogViewerWindow().Show())`. `.Show()`, not `.ShowDialog()` — deliberately non-modal, so a user can keep working in the editor while watching logs stream in. No further composition-root wiring was needed: `LogViewerWindow`'s own constructor already closes over `LoggerSetup.defaultLogsDirectory()` and `LogFileReader.readEntriesSince` directly (`src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/LogViewerWindow.axaml.fs:18`), since a log viewer has no meaningful "unconfigured" state the way `ShellViewModel.ExecuteAsync` or `ConfirmDiscardDirtyDocument` do — there's nothing to inject from `App.axaml.fs` beyond what's already reachable. No TDD cycle for this task, same limitation as Task 4: XAML/menu wiring isn't unit-testable in this codebase (no headless rendering harness), so verification is the same as every prior XAML-only entry — a clean `dotnet build`, which is itself real signal here, since the XAML compiler resolves `Click="..."`/`Name="..."` against real class members and controls at build time.

**Verified:** `dotnet build` clean across the full solution (0 warnings, 0 errors). `dotnet test tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Logging.Tests`: 22/22 green (11 new across Tasks 1-2 — 4 from Task 1's `parseLine` suite, 7 from Task 2's `listAvailableDates`/`readEntriesSince`/`readAllEntries` — over the pre-feature baseline of 11). `dotnet test tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests`: 156/156 green (10 new from Task 3's `LogViewerViewModelTests`, over a 146-test baseline; Tasks 4-5 added no new tests, matching their no-TDD-cycle XAML/wiring scope). A full-solution `dotnet test` was also started to check for regressions elsewhere; it was still running against the `RequiresDocker` SQL Server/PostgreSQL `Integration.Tests` (the same suite every entry this session has flagged as failing without a running Docker daemon) when this entry was written and wasn't waited on to completion, since this task's own two suites — the only ones this feature touches — were already independently confirmed green above and Docker-dependent container tests are unrelated to it either way.

Files changed across all five tasks: `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Logging/LogFileReader.fs` (new), `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Logging/LoggerSetup.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Logging/FSharpQueryStudio.Logging.fsproj`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/LogViewerViewModel.fs` (new), `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/LogViewerWindow.axaml` (new), `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/LogViewerWindow.axaml.fs` (new), `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/MainWindow.axaml`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/MainWindow.axaml.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.fsproj`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Logging.Tests/LogFileReaderTests.fs` (new), `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Logging.Tests/Tests.fs`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Logging.Tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Logging.Tests.fsproj`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests/LogViewerViewModelTests.fs` (new), `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests.fsproj`, `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-12-log-viewer-design.md` (new), `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-12-log-viewer.md` (new), `docs/DEVLOG.md`.

## 2026-08-12 — Fix: five whole-branch-review findings on the log viewer — window ownership, dedup, live-tail sort order, an unguarded read, a redaction blind spot

Each of the log viewer's five build tasks was reviewed and approved individually as it landed, but per-task review only ever sees one task's slice — nobody had looked at the assembled feature end to end, or run the app to actually watch the live tail. A final whole-branch review (dispatched once all five tasks were done, on the most capable available model per this project's own subagent-driven-development convention) caught five real Important-severity issues exactly because it could see across task boundaries. Fixed together in one wave, since all five were small, well-isolated, and independently verifiable.

**Window ownership — an open Logs window silently blocked the app from ever exiting.** `MainWindow`'s `LogsMenuItem` handler called `LogViewerWindow().Show()` — an *unowned* top-level window. Avalonia's default shutdown mode ends the process when the *last* window closes; since the Logs window had no owner, closing the main window (or `File > Exit`) left the process — and the spawned `FSharpQueryStudio.Worker` child, and the Logs window's own `DispatcherTimer` — running invisibly in the background. Every other secondary window in this codebase already uses `ShowDialog(this)`; this was the only unparented one, which also meant it had no z-order relationship to the main window and could sink behind it with no way to bring it back. Fixed with `.Show(this)` instead — an owned window closes automatically with its owner, restoring correct exit behavior and z-ordering while staying non-modal.

**No dedup — repeated clicks opened repeated windows.** Nothing stopped the Help menu from spawning a new `LogViewerWindow` (and a new independently-polling `DispatcherTimer`) on every click. `MainWindow` gained a `mutable logViewerWindow: LogViewerWindow option` field, following the same mutable-state pattern already used for `assetHost`: the handler now `Activate()`s an already-open window instead of creating a duplicate, and the field resets to `None` on the window's own `Closed` event so the next click after closing starts fresh.

**The live tail was, on inspection, not actually usable as a live tail.** `LogViewerViewModel.Entries` reallocates a freshly sorted list on every `Poll()` tick (roughly once a second), and the `DataGrid`'s `ItemsSource` gets replaced wholesale each time — resetting scroll position and row selection continuously. Compounding it: entries sorted oldest-first, so as new lines kept arriving, the newest ones were always below wherever the grid's scroll position had just been reset to — a user could never actually watch new log lines land, which is the entire point of a live tail. Fixed with the cheapest change that solves the real problem without a bigger rearchitecture: both sort sites (`Poll()`'s incremental merge, `SelectedDate`'s static past-date read) now sort `rawEntries` descending by `Timestamp`, so new lines land at the top of the grid, visible without scrolling at all. A genuinely `ObservableCollection`-backed incremental-append rewrite (no full-list replacement, real scroll preservation) would be a further improvement but was deliberately left for later — this fix closes the "I can't watch it" gap without touching the storage/notification model Task 3 already built and tested.

**`AvailableDates` was the one file-read path in this feature with no exception guard.** Every other real I/O call this feature makes (`Poll()`, `SelectedDate`'s static-read branch) already wraps `LogFileReader.readEntriesSince` in a swallow-and-continue `try/with`, per the design's explicit "the log viewer must never be able to crash the app it exists to help debug" requirement — `AvailableDates` called `LogFileReader.listAvailableDates` directly, with nothing between a real `Directory.GetFiles` call (which can throw for permissions or a directory that vanishes between the existence check and the call — the same class of TOCTOU race already flagged and accepted elsewhere in this feature, except this one path had no catch at all) and a property bound straight to the window's date-picker `ComboBox.ItemsSource`. Wrapped in `try ... with _ -> []`, closing the last gap in that requirement. A new test (`AvailableDates doesn't throw when logsDirectory doesn't exist`) covers it — previously the only `LogViewerViewModel` member with no test of its own.

**The redaction leak-scanner couldn't see the new log format it was supposed to be watching.** This feature's first task switched the file sinks from plain text to JSON — which is also what let `Properties` (every logged property, not just the rendered message) start landing on disk for the first time. `Redaction.leakedSecretPattern` — the regex `DiagnosticBundle`'s defense-in-depth scan uses to catch anything `RedactingEnricher` might have missed before it reaches a shareable log bundle — was written for plain-text `key: value`/`key=value` shapes. Against JSON's `"password":"hunter2"` (quotes around both key and value), the pattern's `\s*[:=]\s*` never matched, because the closing `"` after the property name sat directly in its way: a real detection gap opened by a format change nobody had re-pointed the scanner at. Extended the pattern with a second alternative matching the JSON key-value shape, verified against both a real unredacted case (flagged) and an already-redacted case (correctly not flagged, matching the existing plain-text behavior) — the plain-text alternative itself is untouched, so nothing logged via the console sink or before this feature shipped loses coverage.

A scoped re-review of this fix commit (same model tier as the task-level reviews, since it's a small, well-bounded diff) verified all five findings addressed with no new breakage — file:line evidence for every fix, hand-traced the redaction regex against both required cases rather than trusting the implementer's claim.

Nine further Minor findings from the same whole-branch review were deliberately left for later rather than folded into this wave (recorded in this plan's SDD ledger, not repeated here in full): unbounded growth of `rawEntries` with no cap on long-running sessions; pre-upgrade plain-text log files still appearing in the date picker but rendering silently empty; `LogFileReader.readAllEntries` having no production caller (a plan inconsistency — the design assigned it the static-date role, but the ViewModel has to use the injected `readEntriesSince` to stay testable, so `readAllEntries` ended up tested but unused); `IsLive` not surfaced anywhere in the window's XAML, so a user viewing a past date has no visual cue besides the date picker itself that they're not looking at a live view; the "No logs yet" placeholder also covering the case where a filter has emptied an otherwise non-empty log, which reads as more alarming than it is; and a handful of cosmetic gaps (raw `DateTimeOffset.ToString()` in the Time column, lowercase `host`/`worker` in the process filter, no `Fatal` level option). None of these affect correctness or the requirements this feature was built against.

Verified: `dotnet build` clean. `dotnet test tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Logging.Tests`: 24/24 (2 new, covering the redaction fix). `dotnet test tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests`: 157/157 (1 new, covering the `AvailableDates` fix; the sort-order fix updated an existing test's assertion in place rather than adding one).

Files changed: `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/MainWindow.axaml.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/LogViewerViewModel.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Logging/Redaction.fs`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests/LogViewerViewModelTests.fs`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Logging.Tests/DiagnosticBundleTests.fs`.

## 2026-08-12 — Feature: a Welcome tab — real guide content, open at every startup, reachable from the Help menu

User request: "a welcome page with the user guide to this app what should open every time the app starts and also should be available to open by the top menu." Second of four planned sub-projects toward better app observability and polish for real usage (Log Viewer → Welcome Page → Results Export → UI Polish); the Log Viewer entry above already anticipated this one's menu item as the second slot under `_Help`. First-run/first-session context for this app didn't exist at all before this — a fresh workspace opened straight to a blank script with no orientation toward what the app can actually do.

**The brainstorming decision this hinged on: a tab, not a window.** The Log Viewer, the previous sub-project in this same arc, got its own top-level `Window` — a deliberate, considered choice at the time (see that entry's window-ownership fix above for how much machinery a *second* window actually drags in: owner wiring, dedup tracking, its own `Closed` lifecycle). The Welcome guide was explicitly *not* built the same way, and the user was explicit about it wanting to live in the existing tab strip. The design spec ties the two decisions to the same underlying distinction: app *content* the user reads as part of normal workflow belongs in the tab strip, alongside every script tab; separate windows are for diagnostic tooling that sits outside the document-editing workflow, which is what the Log Viewer actually is. A rich, formatted native view (headings, styled sections, clickable actions) was considered and rejected too, in favor of the far cheaper option this app already had lying around: render it as plain `//`-commented F# through the same Monaco editor pane every script tab already uses. Zero new rendering path, at the cost of looking like a comment block instead of a polished screen — an explicit, disclosed trade, not an oversight.

**The seed-selection mechanism, and why the rest of `ShellViewModel` needed zero changes (Task 1, `a04741b`).** `ShellViewModel` already had exactly one hook this feature needed: `createDocument (uri: string)` always built a fresh `DocumentViewModel` seeded from one constant, `newScriptSeed`. Task 1 added a second constant, `welcomeUri = "untitled:Welcome.fsx"` (deliberately reusing the existing `"untitled:"` scheme rather than inventing a new one — `NextUntitledUri`'s own `ScriptN.fsx` probing pattern doesn't match `Welcome.fsx`, so the two coexist with no collision to guard against) and `welcomeGuideSeed`, the real guide text, then rewrote `createDocument`'s one seeding line to go through `seedFor uri` (`if uri = welcomeUri then welcomeGuideSeed else newScriptSeed`) instead of the bare constant. `WelcomeUri` is exposed as a read-only member so both callers this task wires — the composition root's startup call and `MainWindow`'s Help-menu handler — reach the exact same string rather than each carrying its own re-typed literal that could quietly drift apart. Everything downstream of `createDocument` — `OpenDocument`'s open-or-activate dedup logic, `CloseDocument`'s teardown, the tab strip's own rendering — needed not one line of change, because none of it has ever cared *what* seeded a document's initial text, only that it has some. That's the entire reason this shipped as a one-file diff (`ShellViewModel.fs`, 75 lines, all additive) with no ripple into `DocumentViewModel`, the tab strip, or the Monaco bridge.

**Wiring it in (this task, Task 2).** Three mechanical call sites, all pure composition against Task 1's finished surface, no new logic anywhere. `App.axaml.fs`'s composition root calls `mainWindow.ShellViewModel.OpenDocument(mainWindow.ShellViewModel.WelcomeUri) |> ignore` once, right after `SeedConnections` — the same point in startup sequencing, after `ShellViewModel`'s own constructor has already created and activated the initial blank `Script1.fsx`. `OpenDocument`'s existing logic does everything from there: the Welcome document doesn't exist yet, so it's created (seeded via `seedFor`), added to `Documents`, and set as `ActiveDocument` — Welcome active, Script1 open behind it, exactly the requested startup state. `MainWindow.axaml` gained a second item under the `_Help` menu (`_Welcome…`, `Name="WelcomeMenuItem"`, right after `_Logs…`), and `MainWindow.axaml.fs` wires it to the identical `OpenDocument(shellViewModel.WelcomeUri)` call. Reopening an already-open tab just activates it via `OpenDocument`'s existing dedup (no content change); reopening after the user closed it recreates a fresh copy from `seedFor`, not whatever the user last edited it to, since `CloseDocument` drops the `DocumentViewModel` entirely on close.

**The real gap Task 1's design caught before any guide text got written.** The underlying model has a `Persistent` session mode (`DocumentViewModel.SessionMode`) alongside the default `Isolated` one — but a check against the actual running UI, done deliberately before drafting the guide's session-state paragraph rather than assumed from the model alone, turned up **no control anywhere that lets a user switch a document into Persistent mode**: only a read-only `SessionStateDisplay` in the status bar and a Reset Session command exist to interact with session state at all. Writing "every run is Isolated by default, and here's how you'd switch to Persistent" would have described a feature the app doesn't actually expose — worse than describing nothing, since a user would go looking for a control that isn't there. The shipped guide text describes only what's reachable today (Isolated-by-default, Reset Session, the status bar's read-only display) and says nothing about switching modes. Surfacing Persistent mode through real UI, if it's ever wanted, is separate, unstarted follow-up work, not something this guide gets to claim exists ahead of it.

**Guide content, verified against real signatures rather than paraphrased.** The seeded text — all inside `//` comments, including every example call, so F5 on the Welcome tab itself is a harmless no-op rather than a first-run error against a connection that doesn't exist yet — covers running scripts (F5/Cancel, Isolated-by-default sessions), registering and browsing connections (`_Connections > New Connection…`, the Workspace panel's expand-to-browse-schema, the six supported kinds), the actual exported script API (`Connections.sql`/`csv`/`excel`/`rest`, `Results.table`/`text`, checked against `FSharpQueryStudio.Connectors.Connections` and `FSharpQueryStudio.Results`'s real signatures, not remembered ones), the per-tab-scoped Results/Output/Problems/Schema panel (a real, deliberately-built behavior from a recent entry above, worth calling out since it's non-obvious), and `_Help > Logs…` for the Log Viewer this same arc shipped first.

No TDD cycle for this task — XAML/composition-root wiring isn't unit-testable in this codebase (no headless rendering harness), same standing limitation as every other menu-wiring entry above. Task 1 carried its own TDD cycle instead: three `ShellViewModelTests` cases (`OpenDocument(WelcomeUri)` ends up active with real guide content in its text; an ordinary `untitled:ScriptN.fsx` open still gets the plain blank-script seed, unaffected by the new branch; `WelcomeUri` returns the same value across two separate `ShellViewModel` instances, guarding against it ever becoming accidentally instance-specific).

Verified: `dotnet build` clean across the full solution (0 warnings, 0 errors) — real signal here too, since the XAML compiler resolves `WelcomeMenuItem`'s `Name`/`Click` against real members at build time. `dotnet test tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests`: 160/160 green, unchanged from Task 1's own 160 (Task 1 took the suite from 157 → 160 with its three new cases; this task added none, matching its no-TDD XAML/wiring scope). Not visually verified — `claude-in-chrome` unavailable this session, the same standing limitation noted throughout this project's XAML-touching entries.

Files changed across both tasks: `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/ShellViewModel.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.App/App.axaml.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/MainWindow.axaml`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/MainWindow.axaml.fs`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests/ShellViewModelTests.fs`, `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-12-welcome-page-design.md` (new), `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-12-welcome-page.md` (new), `docs/DEVLOG.md`.

## 2026-08-12 — Fix: final whole-branch review of the Welcome tab — the startup push race finally closed, an inertness guard, and a reopen-fresh test

Both Welcome-tab tasks above were reviewed and approved individually as they landed. A final whole-branch review looked at the assembled feature and found one Critical, one Important, and several Minor issues; this wave fixes the Critical, the Important, and two cheap Minors together in one commit.

**Critical: the Welcome guide (and every fresh-startup document) never actually reached the editor.** This is the exact race this project's own `docs/DEVLOG.md` flagged and left open on 2026-08-10 ("One follow-on, smaller issue noticed, not yet fixed... likely fires before Monaco's own `window.chrome.webview` message listener is registered"). `MainWindow`'s constructor pushed the active document to Monaco directly in its `do` block, but `editor-host.html` only attaches its `window.chrome.webview.addEventListener("message", ...)` listener inside the async `require(["vs/editor/editor.main"], function () {...})` callback — which reliably finishes *after* that push, since `postToEditor` swallows both sync and async failures by design (so the drop was silent). Net effect at startup: the tab strip and title bar showed `Welcome.fsx`, but the editor pane stayed empty, still bound to the page's own hardcoded `documentUri = "untitled:Script1.fsx"` default — so the user's first keystroke into what looked like the Welcome tab would have been routed by `RouteTextChanged` into `Script1` instead. The Welcome feature is the first thing that actually depended on this working; nothing before it made the race visible.

Fixed with the handshake the earlier entry's diagnosis specified but never had a task to implement: `editor-host.html`'s `require()` callback now posts a new `editorReady` notification (`postToHost("editorReady", {})`) as its very last statement, immediately after the message listener is registered — the true earliest-safe point. `MainWindow.axaml.fs` dropped the old constructor-time push entirely and added a second, independent `webView.WebMessageReceived.Add(...)` subscription that pushes `DocumentLoaded` only on receiving `editorReady`. This coexists safely with the existing 60-line `match EditorBridge.decode envelope with ...` handler — `.Add` supports multiple subscribers, and `EditorBridge.decode` already returns `Result.Error` (never throws) for the unrecognized `"editorReady"` kind, so neither handler needed to change. The `ActiveDocument`-changed push (tab switches after startup) was untouched — it's correct as-is and was never part of this race.

**Important: nothing guarded that the guide content stays inert.** Added `` the Welcome guide is entirely inert - every non-blank line is a comment `` to `ShellViewModelTests.fs`: splits `WelcomeUri`'s seeded text on newlines and asserts every non-blank line starts with `//`, so a future edit to the guide text can't accidentally introduce a real (non-commented) expression without a test failing.

**Minor (bundled): the close-then-reopen-fresh path — this feature's own headline behavior — had no test.** Added `` closing the Welcome tab and reopening it from OpenDocument gives a fresh, unedited copy ``. As written in the review dispatch it edited the tab's text and closed it directly — but `CloseDocument` on a dirty document requires a configured `ConfirmDiscardDirtyDocument` hook to actually discard (see the existing `` CloseDocument keeps a dirty tab open when no prompt handler is configured `` test above); without it the close is refused and the test would fail on `Assert.True(vm.CloseDocument(...))`. Added `vm.ConfirmDiscardDirtyDocument <- Some(fun _ -> true)` before the close, matching the pattern the other dirty-close tests in this same file already use.

**Minor (bundled): spec wording drift.** `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-12-welcome-page-design.md` line 14 said the Help-menu item recreates the tab "with fresh, unedited content if the user closed **or edited it away**" — but the implemented (and correct) behavior, already stated accurately at the spec's own line 63, just activates an already-open tab without touching its content if it's merely been edited, not closed. Reworded line 14 to match: "reopens it if closed (with fresh, unedited content); if it's still open, just activates it without touching whatever it currently contains."

Verified: `dotnet build` clean across the full solution (0 warnings, 0 errors). `dotnet test tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests`: 162/162 green (2 new, over the 160-test baseline from the two tasks above). Not visually verified — no display in this environment, the same standing limitation noted on every other `editor-host.html`/`NativeWebView` change in this project's history; the test suite and a clean build are the only signal available here.

Files changed: `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Editor/EditorAssets/editor-host.html`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/MainWindow.axaml.fs`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests/ShellViewModelTests.fs`, `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-12-welcome-page-design.md`, `docs/DEVLOG.md`.

## 2026-08-12 — Feature: full-result retention and CSV export, closing the truncation gap the Results grid has always had

Third of four planned sub-projects toward better app observability and polish (Log Viewer → Welcome Page → Results Export → UI Polish). Until this feature, `Results.table` truncated to a preview row count and discarded the rest — the grid could never show, and there was no way to ever get at, the full result of a query. Built as seven tasks off a design spec and implementation plan (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-12-results-export-design.md`, `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-12-results-export.md`), each reviewed as it landed.

**Retention lives in the Worker process, in memory, and nowhere else.** `FSharpQueryStudio.Results.ResultRetention` (Task 1) is a `ConcurrentDictionary<string, RetainedResult>` keyed by document URI, with `set`/`tryGet`/`discard`. It is deliberately independent of `Results.explicit` (which `Results.reset()` already clears before every evaluation) — a retained result survives across runs of *other* tabs and is only ever replaced (a later run of the same tab overwrites its own entry) or evicted (the tab closes). Nothing here is ever written to disk, and it dies with the Worker process by design — this is scratch space for "what the grid is showing right now," not a saved artifact.

**`Results.table` stopped truncating (Task 2).** It now fully materializes every row (`Array.ofSeq`) instead of truncating-and-discarding, so `IsTruncated` is always `false` for this path going forward.

**Wire and dispatch (Tasks 3-4).** `EvaluateParams` gained a required `DocumentUri` field — the running tab's own URI, populated in both Isolated and Persistent session modes — so the Worker knows which tab's retention slot a run's result belongs to. New `ExportCsvParams`/`ResultsDiscardParams` protocol types. `Handlers.runEvaluate` commits a retention write right where `Results.currentEnvelope` is consulted, gated on two conditions together: the envelope must exist (`Some`) *and* its `Kind` must be `ResultKind.Table` — retention is committed at that point, on the explicit envelope's own kind, deliberately not derived from the outer dispatch's envelope after the fact, so the generic FSI-tail-value projection path (`Projection.projectWithCursor`, `results/page`, `currentPaging`) stays completely untouched even on a run where it also happens to produce a Table-kind envelope of its own. New Worker RPCs: `results/exportCsv` (writes the retained result via the pre-existing `CsvExport.write`, a `results/nothingToExport` structured error — not an unhandled exception — when nothing is retained for that URI) and `results/discard` (evicts an entry; always succeeds, a silent no-op on an unknown key, since "close a tab that never ran a query" is a completely ordinary case, not an error).

**App and UI (Tasks 5-6).** `App.ExportService.exportCsv`/`discardResults` are thin RPC wrappers, same shape as every other `*Service` in `FSharpQueryStudio.App`. `ShellViewModel` gained `ExportCsvToFile`/`DiscardResultsAsync` hooks (both `None`-until-configured, the same no-op-when-unconfigured shape every other composition-root hook in this file uses) and `ExportCsvAsync`, the member the UI actually calls. `CloseDocument` calls `DiscardResultsAsync` fire-and-forget immediately after `documents.Remove(doc)` succeeds — inside the `canDiscard` branch, so a refused close (an unconfirmed dirty tab) never evicts anything for a tab that's still open. `App.axaml.fs`'s composition root wires both hooks to `ExportService.exportCsv`/`discardResults`. `MainWindow.axaml` gained an "Export CSV…" toolbar button (`ExportCsvButton`) bound `IsEnabled="{Binding Result.IsTable}"` — the first `IsEnabled` binding anywhere in this codebase's XAML; its click handler in `MainWindow.axaml.fs` opens a `SaveFilePickerAsync` dialog (suggested name `Results.csv`, `*.csv` filter) and calls `ShellViewModel.ExportCsvAsync` with the chosen path, logging a warning on failure rather than showing a dialog of its own — deliberately minimal, matching this button's low blast radius.

**The Results grid needed zero new code — a real, deliberate finding, not an oversight.** `ResultsView.axaml`'s `DataGrid` has bound `ItemsSource="{Binding Result.Rows}"` directly, and been row-virtualized, since Milestone C.12. Once Task 2 stopped truncating at the source, the grid started rendering the complete retained result automatically, the instant it landed, staying performant purely on the DataGrid's own pre-existing UI-level virtualization — no pagination, no "load more," no new binding. Confirming this ahead of time (rather than assuming it and building unnecessary grid changes) was itself part of this plan's design work.

**Deliberate non-goal.** The generic FSI-tail-value projection path — `results/page`, `currentPaging`, `Projection.projectWithCursor` — is untouched by any of the seven tasks. Retention only ever engages for the explicit `Results.table` push path; a script whose result comes from FSI's own tail-value projection never touches `ResultRetention` at all, by the `Kind = ResultKind.Table`-on-the-explicit-envelope gate above.

Verified: `dotnet build` clean across the full solution (0 warnings, 0 errors). `dotnet test` green on every non-Docker-dependent project: `Protocol.Tests`, `Results.Tests`, `Worker.Tests`, `Ui.Tests`, and `Integration.Tests` (filtered to exclude the pre-existing `RequiresDocker` SQL Server/PostgreSQL suites, unrelated to this feature and already flagged as a standing environment limitation in every prior entry that touched `Integration.Tests` in this sandbox).

Files changed across all seven tasks: `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Results/ResultRetention.fs` (new), `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Results/ScriptResults.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Results/FSharpQueryStudio.Results.fsproj`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Protocol/Payloads.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Worker/Handlers.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.App/ExportService.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.App/App.axaml.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/ShellViewModel.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/MainWindow.axaml`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/MainWindow.axaml.fs`, plus the corresponding test files across `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Results.Tests`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Worker.Tests`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui.Tests`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Protocol.Tests`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Integration.Tests`, `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-12-results-export.md` (new), `docs/DEVLOG.md`.

## 2026-08-13 — Feature: generalize `Results.table` to any collection shape, `ConnectorQueryResult`-only `table` renamed to `raw`

Grew out of "UI Polish" brainstorming into its own sub-project (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-12-results-table-generalization-design.md`, `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-12-results-table-generalization.md`). Until this feature, `Results.table` only accepted a `ConnectorQueryResult` — the shape `Connections.sql`/`csv`/`excel` return. Any other collection a script built itself (a list of records, a sequence of tuples, a `Map`, a plain array of arrays) had no path into the Results grid except `Results.text`, which renders it as one opaque structured-printed string, not a real table.

**The rename (Task 1).** The existing `ConnectorQueryResult -> ConnectorQueryResult` function became `Results.raw`, behaviorally unchanged — same body, same pass-through convention.

**The new `Results.table` (Tasks 2-6).** Signature `table (data: obj) : obj`, with a sibling `tableWithColumns (data: obj) (columnNames: string list) : obj` for the explicit-header case (see "An ergonomics bug" below for why these are two functions, not one two-argument function). `data` is materialized to `obj array` (`Seq.cast<obj> |> Array.ofSeq`) and dispatched at runtime, via `Microsoft.FSharp.Reflection`, on the shape of its *first* element — F# has no compile-time way to branch "record vs. tuple vs. nested collection vs. scalar," so this trades static type-checking at the call site for the flexibility requested. Five shapes, each its own `dispatchRows` branch: F# records (reflected field names, declaration order), tuples (require `columnNames`, since tuples carry no field names to infer from), `Map`/`IDictionary` (union of keys across rows, first-seen order, missing keys render blank), nested collections/arrays (row 0 consumed as headers when `columnNames` is omitted; ragged rows pad short and truncate long), and a scalar fallback (single `"Value"` column) for everything else — including `seq<string>`, explicitly excluded from the nested-collection branch so a list of strings renders as one column of strings rather than exploding into characters. Whenever `columnNames` is given, it always wins as the final headers, applied positionally, and its count must match the shape's own inferred width exactly or `table` throws a `"columnNames has N entries but rows have M values"`-style error — the one rule enforced identically across every shape. A `ConnectorQueryResult` passed to the new `table` is a clear, explicit error naming `Results.raw`, checked first (since `ConnectorQueryResult` is itself an F# record, this guard has to run before the generic record-dispatch path or it would silently misdispatch). A non-collection `data` (a bare scalar or record, not wrapped in a sequence) is also a clear error rather than being silently treated as a 1-element sequence.

**Breaking change.** Any existing `.fsx` script that calls `Results.table someConnectorQueryResult` directly must be updated to call `Results.raw someConnectorQueryResult` instead — the new, generalized `Results.table` explicitly rejects a `ConnectorQueryResult` argument with an error naming `Results.raw` as the replacement, rather than silently misinterpreting it as a generic record.

**A regression this branch caught and fixed along the way.** `Worker.Tests`' `HandlersTests.fs` has several embedded FSI scripts (strings evaluated through `Handlers.dispatch`) that called `Results.table` directly on a `ConnectorQueryResult` — exactly the call the rename broke. The plan's own Non-goals section had assumed `Worker.Tests` needed no changes, which turned out to be wrong; four tests started failing once the new `Results.table` began rejecting `ConnectorQueryResult` arguments. Fixed by updating those embedded scripts' `Results.table` calls to `Results.raw`, a human-approved exception to the plan's stated file-structure scope.

**Final whole-branch review fixes (this entry also covers these).** A final review across all seven tasks found four issues, fixed together: the in-app Welcome guide (`ShellViewModel.fs`, reachable via Help menu) still showed `Results.table orders` as an example — the exact call this branch broke — updated to show `Results.raw orders` alongside a `Results.table` example so the guide teaches both functions correctly; the nested-collection dispatch branch was the only one of the five that skipped the `columnNames`-count validation `resolveHeaders` enforces everywhere else, silently padding/truncating to whatever width `columnNames` implied instead of throwing on a genuine mismatch against the first row's actual width — fixed by validating `columnNames`' count against `rawRows.[0].Length` before any padding/truncation, with a new test proving the mismatch throws the standard error; and `dispatchRows`'s doc comment still said "this task only implements the F# record branch... added in later tasks," stale SDD-process narration left over from incremental development — reworded to describe what the function actually does now, with no reference to tasks.

**An ergonomics bug caught by dogfooding after the PR was opened.** `Results.table (data: obj) (columnNames: string list option) : obj` was originally a single two-argument curried function — the exact shape shown in the plan and design spec. A script calling `Results.table rows` with only one argument (matching every other function's single-argument convention, and how anyone porting an old 1-arg `Results.table` call would naturally write it) is completely valid F#: it doesn't error, it curries, and returns a partially-applied function value instead of a table. That function value then became FSI's tail-value `it`, printed via the generic `%A` path as an opaque closure type name (`FSI_0002+it@3`-shaped) — no error, no table, just a confusing string in the Results pane. Fixed by splitting into two one-argument-shaped functions: `table (data: obj) : obj` for the common no-explicit-headers case, and `tableWithColumns (data: obj) (columnNames: string list) : obj` for the explicit-override case — both delegate to the same private `tableImpl`, so no dispatch logic duplicated. This restores `table`'s single-argument pipeline convention (matching `raw`/`text`) and makes the silent-wrong-output footgun structurally impossible: there's no longer a curried second argument to forget.

Verified: `dotnet build` clean across the full solution (0 warnings, 0 errors). `dotnet test tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Results.Tests`: 55/55 (33 baseline + 20 from the new `table` shapes/errors/edge-cases + 1 from the final-review nested-collection validation fix + 1 from the `table`/`tableWithColumns` split proving a 1-argument call never returns a function value). Full solution `dotnet test` green on every non-Docker-dependent project, matching the standing `RequiresDocker` SQL Server/PostgreSQL `Integration.Tests` limitation flagged in every prior entry that touched `Integration.Tests` in this sandbox.

Files changed: `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Results/ScriptResults.fs`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Results.Tests/ScriptResultsTests.fs`, `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Worker.Tests/HandlersTests.fs`, `src/FSharpQueryStudio.Ui/ShellViewModel.fs`, `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-12-results-table-generalization.md` (new), `docs/DEVLOG.md`.

## 2026-08-18/19 — FSAC analysis backend, Tasks 1-9 of 12 (`629db80`..`9508f64`)

First of four planned sub-projects toward using Ionide's tooling (FSAC, Fantomas, FSharp.Analyzers.SDK) to improve the F# coding experience in the Monaco editor — design spec `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-18-fsac-analysis-backend-design.md`, implementation plan `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-18-fsac-analysis-backend.md`, executed via Subagent-Driven Development in an isolated worktree (`fsac-analysis-backend` branch). Replaces `FSharpQueryStudio.Analysis`'s hand-rolled FCS-direct analysis engine with `fsautocomplete` (FSAC), the LSP server Ionide itself uses, as the backend for the Worker's `analysis/*` methods — `Analysis` isn't deleted yet (Task 12), but every method built so far (`check`, `hover`, `definition`, `signatureHelp`) already works end-to-end against the real, bundled `fsautocomplete` 0.83.0.

**Foundation (Tasks 1-5).** `FSharpQueryStudio.Fsac` (new project): `LspTransport` (LSP's `Content-Length:`-framed JSON-RPC over a `Stream`, distinct from `Worker.NdjsonStream`'s own newline-delimited framing for the App↔Worker leg), `LspClient` (request/response correlation by id plus notification dispatch, modeled on `App.WorkerClient`'s `ConcurrentDictionary<_, TaskCompletionSource<_>>` pattern), `FsacProcess` (spawns/supervises the `fsautocomplete` child process, does the `initialize`/`initialized` handshake), and `Prologue` (the `#r`/`open` text prepended to every checked script so `Connections.sql`/`Results.table` resolve unqualified, plus the line-offset math translating between the user's real source and the prologue-prefixed text FSAC actually sees). `fsautocomplete` is bundled as a self-contained build-time restore (`Directory.Build.targets`'s `RestoreFsautocomplete` target) — no network access at app runtime.

**A real MSBuild gap found immediately (Task 1).** `dotnet tool install/update --tool-path` never produces a flat `<tool>.dll` at the tool-path root — the real assemblies always nest under `.store/<pkg>/<ver>/<pkg>/<ver>/tools/<tfm>/any/`, only a native shim exe lands flat. Fixed with an additional `Copy` step flattening that payload up to the build output, verified from a clean `bin`/`obj` rebuild.

**A real bug in the plan's own verbatim test code (Task 3).** `LspClient`'s test-only `Loopback` record declared `ServerWrite: AnonymousPipeClientStream` but `makeLoopback` assigned it an `AnonymousPipeServerStream` — a one-line type-annotation bug in the plan text itself (wiring/semantics were otherwise correct), caught by the implementer refusing to guess and reported, ruled, fixed.

**FsacBridge's six analysis methods, four built so far (Tasks 6-9).** Real `fsautocomplete` 0.83.0 diverged from the plan's design in several load-bearing ways, each found via genuine end-to-end testing against the live process (not assumed) and fixed with review-verified changes:
- FSAC never pushes `textDocument/publishDiagnostics` at all unless the `initialize` request declares that client capability — added to `FsacProcess`.
- Real `publishDiagnostics` carries no `version` field, contradicting the plan's original `(uri, version)`-off-the-notification correlation design — `check` instead correlates against FSAC's own `fsharp/documentAnalyzed` notification (which does carry `{uri, version}`), reading the latest `publishDiagnostics` push for that uri once `documentAnalyzed` confirms the version is settled.
- `Prologue`'s `#r "<path>"` lines corrupted real Windows paths — an unescaped backslash followed by a letter FCS/FSI's string-literal parser treats as an escape code (`\f`, `\n`, `\t`) silently mangled the path, producing a bogus `FS0213` on every single check until backslashes were doubled before interpolation.
- FSAC echoes back a drive-letter-qualified, percent-encoded uri (`file:///d%3A/foo.fsx`) even for a driveless input (`file:///foo.fsx`) — `canonicalizeUri` normalizes both sides wherever a uri is used as a correlation key or compared for same-file identity.
- Any position-based request sent immediately after `didOpen`/`didChange` races FSAC's own asynchronous background parse (confirmed via a reproducible "No line in ... at position ..." error even with correct position math) — `hover`, `definition`, and `signatureHelp` all gate on `awaitDocumentAnalyzed` (the same `documentAnalyzed`-wait `check` already needed) before sending their real request, rather than syncing and immediately asking.
- `hover`'s real markdown response is keyword-prefixed (`val `/`member `/etc.), can span multiple lines for a curried signature, and — critically — has no blank line separating the signature from the following description prose in the `contents` JSON array; the fix reads the array's own real element boundaries (element 0 = signature, later elements = doc/boilerplate) instead of flattening to one string and re-deriving paragraph breaks from whitespace, which had been silently corrupting `Type`/`Signature`/`XmlDoc` for exactly the tested `Connections.sql` case (only `DisplayName` happened to survive, which is why that test stayed green).
- `signatureHelp` only returns non-null with the cursor inside a call's argument list, not on the definition-site identifier the plan's own test assumed — and the real `label` is a curried arrow-type string, not the parenthesized shape the plan's `DisplayName`/`Parameters`/`ReturnType` parsing expected.

**A genuinely load-bearing concurrency bug, caught by review before it could compound (Task 7).** `check` and `hover` both registered a `TaskCompletionSource` under the same `diagnosticsPending[(uri, version)]` key via a plain `ConcurrentDictionary` indexer overwrite. Since `Worker.Program.fs` backgrounds every `analysis/*` method independently with no serialization, a real concurrent `check`+`hover` call for the same document could have `hover`'s registration silently evict `check`'s waiter — `check` would then always hit its own 10s timeout and return `[]`, a silently wrong empty-diagnostics result for a document that may genuinely have compiler errors. Fixed by making `diagnosticsPending` map each key to a list of waiters (resolved together), and extracting a shared `awaitDocumentAnalyzed` helper so `check`/`hover`/`definition`/`signatureHelp` all funnel through one correct implementation instead of copies that could drift. Writing the regression test for this then exposed a second, related race — `syncDocument`'s "detect a respawned FsacProcess client, reset stale state" sequence was itself a non-atomic check-then-act, empirically confirmed via ~2 failures in 16 runs before a `syncLock` fix, 40/40 clean after.

**A deterministic (not flaky) test-infrastructure gap (Task 8).** `FsacBridgeCheckTests`'s crash-recovery test kills the shared `FsacProcess` singleton mid-test; under xUnit's default cross-collection parallelism that kill could land inside a different, concurrently-running test class's in-flight request. Fixed with `tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Fsac.Tests/AssemblyInfo.fs` (`DisableTestParallelization = true`), matching the identical pattern already established in `Execution.Tests`/`Worker.Tests` for the same class of bug.

**A separate, still-open flake, explicitly not fixed here (flagged for follow-up).** `check`'s `awaitDocumentAnalyzed` wait occasionally (~15-30% of full-project test runs, under this sandbox's test-runner load) times out and returns an empty diagnostics list for a document with a real type error. Confirmed independent of Tasks 8-9's own work (reproduces standalone, predates both). Possibly a test-environment artifact (many concurrent `dotnet test`/`fsautocomplete` processes competing for CPU inflating latency past the 10s bound) rather than a real single-Worker-process production risk — unconfirmed either way, tracked for the final whole-branch review rather than guessed at.

Every finding above went through the full task-reviewer → fix-round → scoped-re-review loop before being accepted; several plan-mandated findings (bugs inherited from the plan's own verbatim text, not implementer-introduced) were fixed rather than excused on that basis, per this process's own rule that plan authorship doesn't grade its own work.

**`signatureHelp`'s own fix round.** The `label`-splitting rewrite noted above (`"->"` isn't a parenthesized-shape) initially still naively split on *every* `"->"` in the string, which corrupts `Parameters`/`ReturnType` for any higher-order-function signature — `List.map`, `Seq.filter`, `Array.iter`, or any API taking a callback parameter like `f: (int -> int) -> int`, since the parenthesized function-type parameter's own internal `"->"` gets treated as a separator too. Fixed with `splitTopLevelArrows`, a paren-depth-aware scanner that only treats a `"->"` as a real separator at depth 0, verified against exactly this case. Separately, review flagged that the task's own "non-callable symbol" test passed for the wrong reason (a pre-existing null-position-gate branch, not the `arrowSegments.Length < 2` branch the task actually wrote to detect non-callability) — investigated with two real scenarios against live fsautocomplete (applying a non-function value, over-applying an already-fully-curried function), both of which hit the null-gate first; documented as defensive-only with that evidence in a code comment rather than forcing a contrived test to hit an apparently-unreachable line.

**`complete` (Task 10), landed clean, no fix round.** Fifth of six `FsacBridge` methods. Same background-parse race as `hover`/`definition`/`signatureHelp`, but manifesting differently here — no LSP error, just a silently thin/incomplete completion list. Empirically validated by reverting to a bare `syncDocument` call and re-running the targeted test 5 times: 2/5 runs returned a completion list missing the expected member, confirming the race is real before committing the `awaitDocumentAnalyzed`-gated fix. `parseCompletionLabels` handles both LSP response shapes (`CompletionItem[]` and `CompletionList`), and `AnalysisCompletionResult.Items` stays exactly the flat `string list` the plan's Non-goals call for — no `Protocol.fs` change.

**`rename` (Task 11), landed clean, no fix round — last of the six `FsacBridge` methods.** Calls `textDocument/references` with `includeDeclaration = true`, not `textDocument/rename` — the plan's own Method Mapping calls for this deliberately, since `AnalysisRenameParams`/`AnalysisRenameResult` carry no `newName`/replacement text (this app returns reference spans only; Monaco applies the actual rename text client-side), which is the shape `references` matches and `rename` (a request that computes and returns a `WorkspaceEdit`) doesn't. Both risk patterns established in Tasks 7-10 recurred here exactly as expected and were empirically re-confirmed by reverting each individually: `awaitDocumentAnalyzed` (bare `syncDocument` reproduced the background-parse race, 1/3 runs), and `canonicalizeUri` on the same-file reference filter (a raw uri comparison reproduced Task 8's exact bug — real FSAC's drive-qualified, percent-encoded echo silently filtered out every reference, producing `Some []` instead of the real edits, which masks as "it ran without error" rather than an obvious failure).

`FsacBridge.fs` is now 758 lines across all six methods (`check`/`hover`/`definition`/`signatureHelp`/`complete`/`rename`) — anticipated growth per the plan's own Testing section, not a surprise.

Verified per task: `dotnet test tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Fsac.Tests/FSharpQueryStudio.Fsac.Tests.fsproj` green after every task and fix round (33/33 as of Task 11, modulo the still-tracked pre-existing `check` flake), full solution `dotnet build`/`dotnet test` confirmed clean at multiple checkpoints.

**Task 12 — the cutover, landed clean, no fix round.** `Worker.Handlers`'s six `handleAnalysis*` functions now call `FsacBridge` directly; `FsacBridge.sharedProcess.Shutdown()` wired into `Program.fs`'s exit path; `FSharpQueryStudio.Analysis` and `FSharpQueryStudio.Analysis.Tests` deleted entirely (`git rm -r`); `FSharpQueryStudio.slnx`/`.github/workflows/ci.yml` updated to match. Five real bugs found and fixed beyond the brief's literal text:

- **The most significant one**: `Directory.Build.targets`'s `RestoreFsautocomplete` condition, as the brief specified it (Worker only), was insufficient. `Worker.Tests` (calls `Handlers.dispatch` in-process) and `Integration.Tests` (spawns a *copy* of `Worker.dll` from its own bin folder) each need `FsacTool` in their *own* output directory, not just Worker's real one — `AppContext.BaseDirectory` inside each of those processes is that process's own bin folder. Without it, `FsacBridge.hover`/`check` silently fails to launch `fsautocomplete`, and because `Program.fs`'s `analysis/*` dispatch runs inside a pre-existing fire-and-forget `Task.Run(...) |> ignore`, that failure is swallowed with no error response ever written — the caller just hangs. This was a real, reproducible ~115-second hang-then-crash in `MilestoneBAcceptanceTests`, not a hypothetical. Fixed by adding both test projects to the target's condition. (The fire-and-forget-swallows-exceptions gap itself is pre-existing, unrelated to this migration, and correctly left as a flagged follow-up rather than fixed here.)
- `HandlersTests.fs`'s analysis fixtures reused one `(DocumentUri, Version)` pair across many differently-sourced tests — harmless under the old stateless FCS-direct handlers, but breaks under FSAC's real per-document-identity model (`documentAnalyzed` isn't guaranteed to refire for a repeat version). Root-caused a real 10+ minute test hang; fixed with a `uniqueDocumentUri` helper per test.
- Two `signatureHelp` test assertions and one `complete` test assertion encoded old FCS-era behavior that's inverted or simply gone under real FSAC (signature help only fires inside a call's argument-list, not at a definition/call-site name; completion returns the type `"Connections"`, not a hardcoded `"Connections.sql"`) — rewritten against empirically-verified real behavior.
- `Integration.Tests` had real, not incidental, dependencies on the retired project: `NotebookCells.parse` (a pure `// %%` cell-splitter, zero FCS dependency, that just happened to live in `Analysis`) moved to `Execution`/`Execution.Tests` — its natural home next to `SessionStore.appendFragment`, which actually runs the parsed cells. One test exercising `AnalysisContext.buildForProject` (multi-file FCS project analysis, no FSAC equivalent, an accepted non-goal per this plan's own Self-Review Notes) was deleted with an explanatory comment.

**A serious, escalated concern surfaced during this task's verification — not fixed here, flagged for a required follow-up before treating this migration as fully done.** The `check`-diagnostics-wait flake tracked since Task 8 (previously ~15-30% of full-project test runs, hypothesized as test-runner-load-induced) reproduced 3/3 in a stone-cold, zero-contention isolated run during this task's testing — ruling out the load-only hypothesis. The likely real cause: the fixed 10-second `awaitDocumentAnalyzed` timeout is too tight for a cold-started `fsautocomplete` process's very first parse (spawn + project load + initial analysis), a mechanism with nothing to do with test load and everything to do with cold-start latency — meaning a real user's *first* analysis check right after launching the app could hit this same failure in production. Both this task's reviewer and the controller independently judged this should block calling the whole migration "done," even though it's correctly outside Task 12's own scope to fix (the timing code was deliberately left untouched, per instruction). Recommended follow-up: loosen or make adaptive the cold-start timeout, or add explicit warm-up/retry logic, before shipping.

Manual smoke test (Step 10) was honestly reported as incomplete — this environment has no GUI tooling to interact with the native Avalonia/WebView window, so hover/completion/diagnostics rendering in Monaco was never visually confirmed. Substituted with `Integration.Tests`' `MilestoneBAcceptanceTests`/`MilestoneCAcceptanceTests`, which exercise the same real end-to-end pipeline (`AnalysisService` → real spawned worker → real `FsacBridge` → real `fsautocomplete`) the UI itself calls — the closest available automated proxy, not equivalent to a real visual check.

Verified: full solution `dotnet build FSharpQueryStudio.slnx --configuration Release` — 0 warnings, 0 errors, all 24 projects. `Worker.Tests` 47/47.
